CAT VARC Chapter 07: Arts, Literature and Aesthetics

Reading Comprehension · 204 questions in 48 passages · Teacher book

Paper Tree Educational Studio · v1.0.0

CAT-VARC-P07-001 · Literary form · passage Easy · 453 words · 4 questions

Discussions of the unreliable narrator tend to treat it as a game of detection. A narrator tells us one thing, the novel quietly lets us see another, and the pleasure lies in catching the gap: the jealous husband who insists he is perfectly calm, the child who reports adult quarrels without understanding them. Critics have catalogued the signals by which such narrators give themselves away, the protest that is too insistent, the detail that contradicts a claim made a page earlier, and readers have learned to watch for them. On this account the device is a kind of puzzle, and a reader who has spotted the discrepancy has finished the work the book asked of her. The narrator was wrong; the reader, standing above him, is right.

This picture is not false, but it is thin. It assumes that behind every unreliable account there stands a reliable one, which the attentive reader reconstructs and then keeps. Yet the clues by which we detect a narrator's distortions are supplied by the same narrator. We infer that the husband is jealous from what he notices, what he repeats, what he cannot stop describing; we have no other witness. The true story we assemble is therefore itself an interpretation, built from the materials of the account we have decided to distrust, and we hold it with a confidence that the novel has done nothing to guarantee.

What the best uses of the device achieve, then, is not the replacement of a bad narrator by a good reader but a change in how the reader regards narration as such. Having learned to read one voice against itself, we begin to notice how much we ordinarily grant to voices that give no sign of strain: the calm third-person narrator of a realist novel, the memoirist, the friend recounting a quarrel. None of them is lying, perhaps, but all of them select, arrange and omit. The difference is one of degree rather than kind; the narrator we call reliable is simply one whose selections we have not thought to question. The unreliable narrator makes visible a condition that reliable narration merely conceals.

It must be admitted that not every novel built on an untrustworthy teller rises to this. Some use the device as a trapdoor, a revelation held back for the final chapters that turns everything before it into misdirection. Such books can be thrilling once. On a second reading, however, they tend to go flat, because the only question they posed has been answered. The novels that survive rereading are those in which the distrust never quite settles, in which the reader, having caught the narrator out, is left wondering what she herself has failed to see.

CAT-VARC-P07-001-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)argue that an unreliable narrator's deepest effect is to make readers question the trust they give to all narration.
  2. (B)explain the techniques by which novelists plant the clues that let readers reconstruct the true events behind a distorted account.
  3. (C)condemn novels that hold back a revelation until their final chapters, since such books give their readers no real pleasure.
  4. (D)show that novels told by unreliable narrators are generally inferior to those told by calm and impersonal third-person narrators.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 states the usual view (a puzzle), paragraph 2 calls it 'thin', and paragraph 3 gives the author's position: the best uses of the device bring 'a change in how the reader regards narration as such'. Paragraph 4 is a concession that supports the same point.

Why not (B): This is the 'game of detection' of paragraph 1, which paragraph 2 calls 'thin'; the passage moves beyond it rather than setting out to explain it.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says such books 'can be thrilling once', so they do give pleasure; the paragraph is a concession, not the purpose of the passage.

Why not (D): The passage never ranks the two kinds of novel; paragraph 3 mentions the calm third-person narrator only as a voice we trust too readily, not as a superior model.

Common misconception: Taking the detection view the author reports, or the concession in paragraph 4, for the passage's purpose.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated plainly in paragraph 3; each wrong option is tied to a single paragraph and is easy to rule out. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-001-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, novels that keep a revelation for their final chapters tend to go flat on a second reading because
  1. (A)the clues planted in the early chapters become obvious once the reader knows where to look for them.
  2. (B)the one question they raised has been answered, leaving the reader nothing to wonder about.
  3. (C)their narrators turn out to have been lying, whereas the narrators of better novels merely select and omit.
  4. (D)readers resent having been misled for so long and are unwilling to trust the narrator a second time.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4: 'On a second reading, however, they tend to go flat, because the only question they posed has been answered.' The correct answer restates this reason.

Why not (A): The passage says nothing about clues becoming obvious; it explains the flatness by the answered question alone.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 contrasts lying with selecting only when discussing ordinary narrators; that contrast is not the reason given for these novels going flat.

Why not (D): Resentment is never mentioned; paragraph 4 gives a different reason, that 'the only question they posed has been answered'.

Common misconception: Supplying a plausible reason from general reading experience instead of the one the passage states.

Difficulty: The reason is given in a single sentence introduced by 'because'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-001-Q3 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The observation that the clues to a narrator's distortions 'are supplied by the same narrator' (paragraph 2) implies that
  1. (A)unreliable narrators usually intend their distortions to be noticed by an attentive reader of the novel.
  2. (B)novelists who employ unreliable narrators ought to include a second, trustworthy voice to settle what happened.
  3. (C)the reader can confirm the true story by comparing the narrator's account with the account of another character.
  4. (D)the reader's version of what really happened rests on testimony that the reader has already decided to doubt.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2 goes on to say that the true story we assemble is 'built from the materials of the account we have decided to distrust'. The correct answer states this consequence.

Why not (A): The narrator's intentions are never discussed; the point concerns where the reader's evidence comes from, not what the narrator means to reveal.

Why not (B): The passage makes no recommendation to novelists; it describes the reader's position without proposing a remedy.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: 'we have no other witness'.

Common misconception: Reading the remark as a claim about the narrator's intentions rather than about the reader's evidence.

Difficulty: The implication is spelled out in the sentences that follow the quoted phrase. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-001-Q4 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following situations is most closely analogous to the effect that the author attributes to the best uses of the unreliable narrator?
  1. (A)A detective who exposes a lying suspect closes the case, confident that the full sequence of events is now known.
  2. (B)A juror who catches one witness contradicting himself concludes that all the remaining witnesses must therefore be telling the whole truth.
  3. (C)A juror who catches one witness contradicting himself starts to ask what the apparently consistent witnesses may also have left out.
  4. (D)A reader who discovers that one memoir contains invented episodes resolves never to trust any memoir again.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says that having learned to read one voice against itself, 'we begin to notice how much we ordinarily grant to voices that give no sign of strain'. The juror who turns his scrutiny to the consistent witnesses mirrors this extension of attention from one untrustworthy voice to all voices.

Why not (A): This matches the view in paragraph 1, where the reader who spots the discrepancy 'has finished the work'; the author rejects that as the device's best effect.

Why not (B): This replaces a bad witness with trusted ones, which is the 'thin' detection picture; the author's point is that distrust should extend to voices 'that give no sign of strain'.

Why not (D): The effect the author describes is awareness that every narrator will 'select, arrange and omit' (paragraph 3), a sharper attention to all voices, not a wholesale rejection of one genre.

Common misconception: Choosing the detective or the first juror, who stop at catching one liar, which is the view the author calls thin.

Difficulty: Two wrong options reproduce the view the author reports; the correct answer requires matching the extension of distrust in paragraph 3, and the third wrong option overshoots it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-002 · Literary form · passage Easy · 432 words · 4 questions

A persistent habit of literary talk treats the short story as a smaller novel: the same materials of character, setting and incident, simply supplied in lesser quantities. On this view the story is where writers practise before attempting the larger form, and a collection of stories is a kind of portfolio of novels not yet written. The habit is reinforced by publishers, who have long regarded collections as harder to sell, and by critics who praise a story for being 'as rich as a novel', as though richness of that kind were the measure of success. Prizes and anthologies have sometimes followed suit, treating a writer's stories as preparation and the first novel as the real debut.

The comparison misunderstands where a short story does its work. A novel accumulates; it earns its effects through duration, letting the reader live alongside its people for enough days that change in them can be felt as change. A story has no such time to spend. What it has instead is selection, and selection pressed hard enough becomes a kind of meaning in itself. When a story shows a woman packing a suitcase, pausing over a single photograph and putting it back in the drawer, the eleven years of marriage that the story never describes are present precisely because they are withheld. The reader does not receive them; she supplies them, and the supplying is the experience.

This is why expanding a fine story so rarely improves it. Give the woman a history, a sister to confide in, a chapter on the courtship, and the photograph becomes one detail among many rather than the point on which everything turns. The gaps that had been doing the work are filled with information, and information, however well written, is less charged than implication. The reader who had been supplying the marriage is now simply told about it, and being told is a lesser kind of participation. The story's brevity was not a limitation it laboured under but the condition of its force.

None of this means that stories and novels never meet. Some writers have plainly used stories as sketches, and more than one long novel began as a few pages its author could not leave alone. Nor is brevity a virtue in itself; a weak story can hide behind its shortness, gesturing at depths it has not earned. The claim is narrower: that when a story succeeds on its own terms, it succeeds by a method the novel mostly cannot use, and that judging it by the novel's standards will reliably mistake its strengths for omissions.

CAT-VARC-P07-002-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The example of the woman packing a suitcase (paragraph 2) serves primarily to
  1. (A)demonstrate that short stories most often take domestic life and the breakdown of marriages as their subject.
  2. (B)show that a story grows more moving once the backgrounds of its characters have been explained in some detail.
  3. (C)illustrate how a story makes an unstated history felt through what it chooses to show and leave out.
  4. (D)suggest that photographs and other personal objects are the most effective details a writer can place in a scene.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The example comes straight after the claim that 'selection pressed hard enough becomes a kind of meaning in itself', and the passage says the eleven years 'are present precisely because they are withheld'. It illustrates meaning made by omission.

Why not (A): One example cannot establish what stories most often concern, and the passage makes no claim about the usual subjects of stories.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 argues the reverse: giving the woman 'a history' makes the photograph 'one detail among many'.

Why not (D): The photograph matters because of what it implies, not because objects of that kind are better than others; the passage ranks no types of detail.

Common misconception: Reading the example literally, as a point about subject matter or objects, rather than about method.

Difficulty: The function is announced by the sentence that introduces the example. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-002-Q2 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a novel earns its effects chiefly through
  1. (A)the very large amount of background information it supplies about the earlier lives of its characters.
  2. (B)the length of time over which the reader lives alongside its characters and watches them change.
  3. (C)the pressure of selection, which turns a few chosen details into a kind of meaning.
  4. (D)the variety of minor characters and subplots that its greater length allows it to sustain.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: 'A novel accumulates; it earns its effects through duration, letting the reader live alongside its people for enough days that change in them can be felt as change.'

Why not (A): Information is discussed only in paragraph 3, as what weakens an expanded story; it is never named as the novel's source of effect.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 assigns selection to the short story: 'What it has instead is selection'. The novel is contrasted with it.

Why not (D): Subplots and minor characters are not mentioned; the passage names duration, not variety, as the novel's resource.

Common misconception: Confusing the novel's method with the story's, since the two are described in adjacent sentences.

Difficulty: The answer is a direct paraphrase of one sentence; the main lure is the story's method in the next line. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-002-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author would most likely regard a critic's praise of a story as 'as rich as a novel' as
  1. (A)evidence that most critics do not read short stories with any real attention or care.
  2. (B)an accurate account of what the best short stories manage to achieve within their few pages.
  3. (C)a sign that publishers have at last begun to value collections of short stories as highly as novels.
  4. (D)a compliment that measures the story by a standard that belongs to a different form.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 cites the phrase as an example of the habit of treating the story as a smaller novel, and paragraph 4 concludes that 'judging it by the novel's standards will reliably mistake its strengths for omissions'. The praise is such a judgement.

Why not (A): The passage criticises the standard such critics apply, not their attentiveness, and a claim about most critics goes beyond anything stated.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the praise treats 'richness of that kind' as the measure of success 'as though' it were, signalling disagreement; the story's force lies elsewhere.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says publishers regard collections as 'harder to sell' and that the habit is 'reinforced' by them, so the praise belongs to the same habit, not to a change in it.

Common misconception: Taking the praise at face value because it sounds generous to the story.

Difficulty: The answer requires linking a phrase in paragraph 1 to the conclusion in paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-002-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim about expanding fine stories?
  1. (A)Readers comparing admired stories with later full-length versions by the same writers mostly found the longer versions stronger, because of the added history.
  2. (B)Publishers continue to report that collections of short stories sell fewer copies than novels by the same writers.
  3. (C)Several admired novels are known to have grown out of rough, unfinished short pieces that their authors went on revising, sometimes for many years, before finally publishing them.
  4. (D)Writers who began with short stories and later turned to novels often say that the shorter form taught them economy.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 claims that 'expanding a fine story so rarely improves it' because filling the gaps with information weakens implication. Evidence that expanded versions are generally found more powerful, precisely because of the added history, strikes at both the claim and its reason.

Why not (B): Sales concern the trade's habit in paragraph 1, not whether expansion improves a story; the fact is irrelevant to the claim.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 already concedes that 'more than one long novel began as a few pages', so this is consistent with the argument and does not show that expansion improves fine stories.

Why not (D): This supports, if anything, the value of the story's method and says nothing about whether expanded stories are better or worse.

Common misconception: Choosing the novels that grew from short pieces, which the author has already conceded.

Difficulty: One wrong option restates a concession, which tempts readers who do not check what the author already allows. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-003 · Literary form · passage Medium · 449 words · 4 questions

Among serious readers there is a settled suspicion of the happy ending. A novel that closes with a wedding, a reconciliation or a recovered fortune is assumed to have flinched at the last moment, to have given its audience what it wanted rather than what its story required. An ending in death, separation or failure, by contrast, is credited with courage. The assumption behind the contrast is rarely stated but easy to reconstruct: life does not resolve itself neatly, so a book that resolves neatly must be telling a comforting lie. The suspicion is old enough to have become a reflex, and few readers who share it could say where they acquired it.

There is something to this, since the history of popular fiction is crowded with endings that arrive from outside the story, the unexpected legacy, the villain who repents in a paragraph, the rival who conveniently dies, and readers are right to feel cheated by them. Such endings resemble a rescue by a passing stranger: welcome, perhaps, but unconnected with anything the characters have done. But the fault in such endings is not their happiness. It is that nothing in the preceding chapters made them necessary. A catastrophe imposed in the same way, a heroine killed by a runaway carriage because the author wanted the book to feel weighty, is no less arbitrary and, being arbitrary, no more truthful.

The prejudice persists, I think, because it confuses two things that endings do. Every ending closes; it marks the point after which the story will not be told. Some endings also console; they leave the reader reassured about the world the book has shown. Closure is a formal necessity, since a story must stop somewhere, while consolation is a choice. The suspicious reader notices that happy endings console, objects to the consolation, and then mistakes the objection for an argument against closure itself, as though the honest book would be the one that refused to finish. Yet an unhappy ending closes just as firmly; it simply closes on a different note, and that note can be just as comfortably familiar to readers who have learned to expect gloom from serious fiction.

The better test, then, is an internal one: an ending is honest when it is the place towards which the book's own pressures have been carrying it, so that a reader looking back can see how the earlier pages prepared it, whether that place is a marriage or a grave. By this test some happy endings are among the hardest-won things in fiction, and some tragic ones are merely fashionable. The question to ask of an ending is not whether it pleases but whether it follows.

CAT-VARC-P07-003-Q1 · Structure / function · EasyMCQ
The heroine 'killed by a runaway carriage' (paragraph 2) is mentioned in order to
  1. (A)prove that tragic endings are in general less honest than the happy endings that serious readers distrust.
  2. (B)show that an unhappy ending forced on a story from outside is as arbitrary as a contrived happy one.
  3. (C)suggest that sudden accidental deaths are seldom believable in novels whose stories are set in earlier centuries.
  4. (D)illustrate the kind of courageous ending that serious readers are right to admire in a novel.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says the fault of contrived happy endings is that 'nothing in the preceding chapters made them necessary', and then offers the carriage death as 'A catastrophe imposed in the same way', 'no less arbitrary'. The example extends the fault to unhappy endings.

Why not (A): The author says such a catastrophe is 'no more truthful', not less, and paragraph 4 allows that some tragic endings are honest; the option overstates and ranks.

Why not (C): The period setting is incidental; the point is that the death is imposed 'because the author wanted the book to feel weighty', not that accidents are unbelievable.

Why not (D): This is the view of paragraph 1 that the example is meant to unsettle; the carriage death is called 'arbitrary', not courageous.

Common misconception: Taking the example as a general verdict on tragic endings rather than as a parallel case.

Difficulty: The phrase 'imposed in the same way' states the function directly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-003-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
As used in paragraph 3, an ending is said to 'console' when it
  1. (A)makes up to the reader for the sadness and suffering of the earlier chapters.
  2. (B)rewards the characters the reader likes and punishes those the reader has come to dislike.
  3. (C)marks clearly the point beyond which the story will not go on being told.
  4. (D)leaves the reader reassured about the world that the novel has depicted.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 3 defines the term: 'Some endings also console; they leave the reader reassured about the world the book has shown.' The correct answer paraphrases this definition.

Why not (A): This is an everyday sense of consoling, but the passage defines the word differently, as leaving the reader 'reassured about the world the book has shown'.

Why not (B): Rewards and punishments are not mentioned; the passage defines consolation by its effect on the reader's view of the world.

Why not (C): This is what the passage calls closing: 'it marks the point after which the story will not be told'. The author separates it from consoling.

Common misconception: Choosing the definition of closure, which sits in the preceding sentence, or the everyday sense of the word.

Difficulty: The passage's own definition must be preferred over the ordinary sense, and closure is defined in the adjacent sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-003-Q3 · Except / not supported · MediumMCQ
The author makes each of the following claims EXCEPT that
  1. (A)readers ought to prefer novels that end in marriage or reconciliation to novels that end in failure.
  2. (B)some endings that arrive from outside the story deserve the reader's feeling of having been cheated.
  3. (C)every story, whatever its tone or subject, has to stop at some point and leave the rest untold.
  4. (D)an unhappy ending can meet the expectations of some readers as predictably as a happy one can.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The author's test in paragraph 4 is indifferent to tone: an ending is honest 'whether that place is a marriage or a grave'. Nowhere does the author rank happy endings above unhappy ones; the passage defends happy endings only against blanket suspicion.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says readers 'are right to feel cheated' by endings that 'arrive from outside the story', so the author does make this claim.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 calls closure 'a formal necessity, since a story must stop somewhere', so the author makes this claim.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the unhappy note 'can be just as comfortably familiar to readers who have learned to expect gloom', so the author makes this claim.

Common misconception: Mistaking the defence of happy endings against prejudice for a preference for them.

Difficulty: The unsupported option is a plausible overextension of the author's sympathy for happy endings. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-003-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following can be inferred about the 'suspicious reader' described in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)Such a reader is right to object to consolation in fiction but mistaken in admiring unhappy endings for their courage.
  2. (B)Such a reader believes that every serious novel must end in death, separation or failure if it is to count as honest fiction.
  3. (C)Such a reader treats a dislike of reassurance as if it were an objection to bringing a story to a definite end.
  4. (D)Such a reader consciously prefers novels that refuse to finish at all, ranking them above both happy and tragic ones.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3: the suspicious reader 'objects to the consolation, and then mistakes the objection for an argument against closure itself'. The correct answer restates this confusion of the two functions the author has just distinguished.

Why not (A): The author never endorses the objection to consolation, calling consolation merely 'a choice'; the option assigns the author a verdict the passage does not give.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says unhappy endings are 'credited with courage', but a requirement on every serious novel is far stronger than anything attributed to the reader.

Why not (D): The phrase 'as though the honest book would be the one that refused to finish' is the author's account of where the reader's confusion leads, not a preference the reader holds; paragraph 1 shows this reader admiring tragic endings.

Common misconception: Reading the author's 'as though' clause as a description of the reader's actual taste.

Difficulty: Two wrong options are close to the text: one turns the author's reductio into the reader's stated preference, the other grants the author a verdict on consolation that is never given. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-004 · Literary form · passage Medium · 480 words · 4 questions

The line between literary and genre fiction is usually drawn in terms of freedom. The literary novel, it is said, invents its own shape, while the crime novel, the romance and the tale of space travel arrive with their shapes already supplied: a body in the first chapter, an obstacle between the lovers, a voyage, a crisis and a return. To write in a genre is, on this account, to fill in a form someone else designed, and the best a genre writer can hope for is to fill it in with unusual skill. Critics who accept this account tend to praise a crime novel by saying that it rises above its genre, as though the genre were something to be escaped.

The account has the logic of constraint backwards. Nobody thinks a sonnet a lesser poem because its length and rhyme scheme were fixed before the poet began. We understand that the fixed form is what makes certain effects possible: a turn in the ninth line registers as a turn only because the reader knows where turns are expected. Genre conventions work in much the same way. A reader of detective stories knows that the culprit must be among the people introduced, that the clues must be fair, that a solution will come. These expectations are not a cage around the writer but an instrument she can play, and the slightest departure from them, a detective who is wrong, a solution that satisfies the law but not the reader, is felt with a precision no unconventional novel can command, because the unconventional novel has no settled expectation to depart from.

This is also why devoted readers of a genre are often its most exacting critics. Having read a hundred novels built on the same frame, they are alert to the smallest variation in it, much as a chess player who knows an opening thoroughly notices a single unusual move that a novice would pass over. Such readers can tell at once whether a twist is earned or merely borrowed, and they are rarely generous to a writer who mistakes one for the other. Their pleasure is not the passive pleasure of repetition but the active one of comparison.

None of this rescues every genre book. Conventions harden into formula when a writer treats them as a checklist to be completed rather than an expectation to be worked against, and a great deal of genre fiction is written in exactly this way. But the same is true of literary fiction, which has its own conventions, the quiet revelation in the final paragraph, the fractured chronology, the unnamed narrator, and its own writers who complete them dutifully. The useful distinction is not between books that have conventions and books that lack them, since none lack them, but between books that do something with their conventions and books that merely obey them.

CAT-VARC-P07-004-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a turn in the ninth line of a sonnet registers as a turn because
  1. (A)the reader already knows the point in the poem at which a turn is expected.
  2. (B)readers of sonnets are taught to look for a contradiction of the poem's opening.
  3. (C)the ninth line is by tradition the longest and most emphatic line of the poem.
  4. (D)the poet abandons the rhyme scheme at that line so as to mark the change clearly.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2: 'a turn in the ninth line registers as a turn only because the reader knows where turns are expected'. The correct answer restates this.

Why not (B): The passage does not describe what a turn contains or how readers are taught; it says only that they know 'where turns are expected'.

Why not (C): Line length is never mentioned; the passage says only that the length of the whole poem is fixed.

Why not (D): The passage says nothing about abandoning the rhyme scheme; it attributes the effect to the reader's expectation.

Common misconception: Supplying outside knowledge about sonnets instead of the reason the passage gives.

Difficulty: The reason is stated in one clause beginning 'only because'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-004-Q2 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
The author lists 'the fractured chronology' and 'the unnamed narrator' (paragraph 4) in order to
  1. (A)illustrate the inventions by which literary novels create their own shape instead of inheriting one.
  2. (B)show that literary fiction also depends on inherited conventions that can be followed mechanically.
  3. (C)argue that literary fiction has become more formulaic than any of the popular genres it looks down on.
  4. (D)identify techniques that genre writers have recently borrowed from the literary novel.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says the hardening of conventions into formula is 'the same' in literary fiction, 'which has its own conventions', and gives these devices as examples, adding that some writers 'complete them dutifully'. They support the conclusion that no books lack conventions.

Why not (A): This is the view of paragraph 1 that the author rejects; the devices are offered as conventions, and the author adds that some writers 'complete them dutifully', so they are not inventions.

Why not (C): The author says 'the same is true' of literary fiction, an equivalence, not a claim that it is more formulaic than every genre.

Why not (D): Borrowing between the two is never discussed; the devices are presented as literary fiction's own conventions.

Common misconception: Reading the examples as an attack that ranks literary fiction below genre fiction.

Difficulty: The function depends on the 'same is true' clause; one wrong option overstates it into a ranking. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-004-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
It can be inferred that the author regards the pleasure of the devoted genre reader as
  1. (A)confined to checking whether a writer has followed every rule of the genre correctly.
  2. (B)lesser than the pleasure of meeting an unconventional novel for the first time without expectations.
  3. (C)depending on close knowledge of the conventions against which a particular book can be measured.
  4. (D)arising chiefly from the comfort of meeting familiar situations and outcomes once again.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says devoted readers, having read 'a hundred novels built on the same frame', notice the smallest variation, and that their pleasure is 'the active one of comparison'. Comparison requires knowledge of the frame against which each book is measured.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 describes alertness to 'the smallest variation', not to compliance; checking rules is closer to the formula writer's 'checklist' in paragraph 4.

Why not (B): The passage never compares these pleasures; it says only that the unconventional novel cannot make departures felt so precisely.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the pleasure is 'not the passive pleasure of repetition', which this option describes.

Common misconception: Equating familiarity with repetition, which the passage explicitly rules out.

Difficulty: Requires inferring what 'comparison' presupposes; one wrong option is the reading the passage denies. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-004-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
The claim that a departure from convention in a detective story 'is felt with a precision no unconventional novel can command' depends on which of the following assumptions?

  2. (B)Detective stories that break their conventions are more highly valued than detective stories that keep them.
  3. (C)Most readers of detective stories have read more widely than most readers of literary fiction.

Why not (B): The claim concerns how precisely a departure is felt, not how much the book is valued; the argument needs no ranking of books.

Why not (C): Breadth of reading is not needed; even a reader of a few detective stories knows that 'the culprit must be among the people introduced'.

Common misconception: Picking an option about readers' qualities instead of the link between expectation and effect that the reason requires.

Difficulty: The assumption must be extracted from a 'because' clause; the wrong options offer alternative explanations that the argument does not need. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-005 · Literary form · passage Medium · 455 words · 4 questions

The commonest objection to free verse is that it is merely prose with the margins moved. Take away the rhyme and the regular metre, the objection runs, and nothing distinguishes a poem from a paragraph except the typographer's decision to stop each line short of the edge of the page. If that decision is arbitrary, the poem is a pretence; if it is not, the objector would like to know what principle governs it, and a vague appeal to the poet's ear or instinct will not satisfy him.

The question deserves a better answer than poets usually give it, and the principle, where there is one, is that the line is a unit of attention. A reader pauses, however slightly, at the end of a line, and in that pause whatever the line has said is briefly complete. A sentence broken across two lines is therefore read twice: once as the line presents it and once as the sentence finally does. Consider a line that ends 'she said she would not leave', followed by one that begins 'the house until the rain had stopped'. For the length of the pause the woman refuses to leave at all; the next line shrinks the refusal to a matter of weather. Neither reading cancels the other; the poem holds the larger refusal and the smaller one together, and prose, which delivers its sentences whole, cannot easily do this.

Line breaks also govern pace, since a poem made of short lines slows the reader, weighting each word with a pause, while long lines carry her forward faster than the sense alone would. Where metre once supplied a regular beat against which a poet could set the natural stresses of speech, the free verse poet works with the silence at the ends of lines, which is a cruder instrument than metre but not a negligible one, and which has the compensating advantage that it can be varied from line to line without breaking any rule.

None of this shows that the objection is always wrong. A good deal of what is published as free verse is broken into lines for no reason a reader could recover, and in such cases the objection is simply correct: the lines could be run together without loss. A fair test is exactly that: set the poem out as prose, read it again, and ask what has gone. If nothing is lost, if no meaning that the breaks created has vanished and no pace has been flattened, then the breaks were decoration. What the test cannot do is condemn the form as such, since in the poems that pass it the breaks turn out to have been doing work that the prose version cannot preserve.

  2. (B)agreeing to leave the house as soon as the rain outside has finally stopped falling.
  3. (C)refusing to leave at all, until the next line narrows her refusal to the weather.
  4. (D)speaking insincerely, so that the reader begins to doubt whether she means what she says.
Answer and explanation

Why not (B): This is the reading the whole sentence finally gives; paragraph 2 says that during the pause 'the woman refuses to leave at all'.

Why not (D): Sincerity is never raised; the example concerns how the line break changes the scope of the refusal.

Common misconception: Choosing the meaning of the completed sentence instead of the meaning the single line has during the pause.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in one sentence; the lure is the full-sentence reading. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-005-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
As used in paragraph 4, line breaks that were 'decoration' are breaks that

  2. (B)give a text the look of verse while adding nothing to its meaning or its pace.
  3. (C)make a poem visually attractive on the page, which the author treats as a legitimate secondary aim.
  4. (D)enrich a poem's language with ornamental words that make its images more vivid.

Answer: (B)

The sentence defines the term by its condition: 'If nothing is lost, if no meaning that the breaks created has vanished and no pace has been flattened, then the breaks were decoration.' Decorative breaks are those that contribute neither meaning nor pace.

Why not (C): The author uses 'decoration' as a verdict of failure ('then the breaks were decoration') and nowhere calls appearance a legitimate aim.

Why not (D): The word refers to the breaks, not to the poem's vocabulary; ornamental diction is never discussed.

CAT-VARC-P07-006 · Literary form · passage Medium · 427 words · 4 questions

Few critical preferences are as widely shared as the preference for round characters over flat ones. A round character, in the usual formulation, has an inner life complex enough that it can change, contradict itself and still remain recognisable; a flat character is built around a single trait and can be summed up in a sentence. The first is praised as lifelike, the second tolerated as a convenience, useful for filling a drawing room or delivering a message but not to be mistaken for the real achievement of fiction.

Yet the preference rests on an assumption that deserves scrutiny: that the novel's central business is the representation of individual inner life. That has been the business of one powerful tradition, but it is not the only thing novels do. Many of the novels readers return to are as much about a society as about a self, and a society is felt in fiction partly through the people who never change: the aunt who has said the same thing at every family dinner for thirty years, the official for whom every request is a breach of procedure. Their fixity is not a failure of imagination. It is how the novel registers the pressure of a world that does not bend, against which its few changing figures can be measured.

Flat characters also carry much of fiction's comedy, which depends on repetition. A man who responds to every misfortune by consulting the same almanac is funny the third time precisely because he could not have done otherwise. Deepen him, give him a moment of doubt about the almanac, and the joke dissolves into pathos; this may be a gain, but it is a different book. There is, too, a case that flatness is closer to ordinary experience than critics allow. Most of the people we know, we know only by a few habits and phrases, and a novel that rendered every acquaintance in the round would misrepresent how other people actually appear to us, however much it flattered our sense of their depth.

This is not to invert the hierarchy. A novel peopled entirely by flat characters would be a cartoon, and the moment when a figure we had thought simple reveals unexpected depth remains one of fiction's great effects, though it depends on our having first taken him as simple. The point is that flat and round are not grades of achievement but different tools, suited to different tasks, and a criticism that rewards only one of them will misread every novel in which both are at work.

  2. (B)Flat characters do work of their own in fiction, so treating roundness as the only mark of achievement misreads many novels.
  3. (C)Flat characters are more lifelike than round ones, because people in ordinary life are known to us only by their habits.
  4. (D)The best novels are those that portray a whole society rather than the inner life of a single individual.
Answer and explanation

Paragraphs 2 and 3 give flat characters functions (registering a society, comedy, fidelity to how others appear), and paragraph 4 concludes that flat and round 'are not grades of achievement but different tools' and that a criticism rewarding only one 'will misread every novel in which both are at work'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 4 says that one particular effect depends on first taking a figure as simple; it does not say round characters in general succeed only this way.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 offers this only as 'a case that' flatness is closer to experience, and paragraph 4 says 'This is not to invert the hierarchy'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says the inner-life tradition is 'one powerful tradition' among others; the author does not rank the two kinds of novel.

Common misconception: Choosing the option that inverts the hierarchy, which the author explicitly declines to do.

Difficulty: Each wrong option overstates one supporting point; the correct answer needs the conclusion of paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-007 · Literary form · passage Difficult · 642 words · 5 questions

The word from which the essay takes its name once meant an attempt or a trial, the act of testing something by weighing it, and the form has never quite outgrown that sense. An essay, in its original spirit, is not the report of a conclusion already reached but the record of a mind trying a thought to see whether it will bear weight. It may change direction halfway through, concede more than it intended, or end with a question sharper than the one it started with. These are not lapses. They are the form doing what it was invented to do, and the reader of such an essay is permitted to overhear a mind in conversation with itself rather than merely to receive the outcome of that conversation.

It is worth noticing how far the essay most people now encounter has moved from this. The essay that is taught in schools and demanded in universities opens with a thesis, marshals evidence in its favour, dispatches objections and restates the thesis at the close. It is, in effect, a miniature treatise, and its virtues are those of the treatise: clarity of claim, economy of proof, a structure the reader can grasp in advance. These are real virtues, and there are good reasons to teach them, not least that a writer who cannot state a claim plainly is in no position to test it. But the result is that a generation of writers has learned to regard any movement of thought within a piece of writing as a defect of planning, something to be removed in revision so that the finished text looks as though its conclusion had been visible from the first sentence.

What is lost is not merely a style. The provisional essay offers something the treatise structurally cannot: a view of how a conclusion is reached, including the paths that were tried and abandoned. A reader who watches a writer test an idea against an example, find that it fails, and adjust it, learns not only where the writer arrived but how much the arrival cost and what it excluded. She is reminded, too, that the conclusion was reached by someone who could have been wrong and who knows it. The treatise presents its conclusion as if it were the only place the evidence could lead; the essay, by showing the alternatives it passed through, leaves the reader better placed to disagree. In this sense its apparent modesty is a form of candour about the limits of its own claims.

The same candour explains the essayist's much criticised first person. The 'I' of the essay is often taken for self-display, and sometimes it is exactly that. More often it marks the scope of a claim: this is how the matter looks from here, to someone with this experience, and the reader is invited to test it against her own. The impersonal voice of the treatise, by contrast, claims a standpoint from nowhere in particular, and so makes its claims harder to locate and therefore harder to contest. Its objectivity is sometimes real, but it is always also a manner, and a manner can be adopted by writers whose claims have not earned it.

None of this licenses formlessness. Provisionality is easily counterfeited, and the meandering essay that circles its subject without ever risking a claim is as familiar as the rigid one, and less useful. A thought cannot be tested unless it is first stated with enough definiteness to fail. A genuine attempt is one that might not succeed, and an essay that cannot fail because it never claims anything has not attempted anything at all. The essay at its best is therefore not the opposite of argument but argument conducted in the open, where the reader can see each step taken and judge for herself whether it holds.

CAT-VARC-P07-008 · Literary form · passage Difficult · 631 words · 5 questions

Many of the long novels of the nineteenth century first reached their readers not as books but in instalments, issued monthly or weekly over a year or more, in magazines or in separate parts. Modern criticism has tended to treat this fact as an extenuating circumstance. When such a novel sprawls, introduces a character it later forgets, or closes a chapter on a contrived moment of suspense, the serial is invoked as an excuse: the author was writing to deadline, paid by length, obliged to bring readers back next month. The implication is that the real novel, the one the author would have written in better conditions, lies somewhere beneath the one we have, and that serial publication is a kind of noise to be filtered out.

The trouble with this view is that it treats the serial only as a set of pressures, when it was also a set of possibilities, and many of the qualities we now admire in these books grew from it. A novel read over eighteen months is lived with in a way that a novel read over a week is not. Its characters age alongside their readers; its crises arrive amid the readers' own. The large casts and interwoven plots that later critics found unwieldy were in part a response to this rhythm, since a reader returning after a month's absence needed several lines of interest to pick up, and recurring figures with distinctive habits of speech helped her find her place. What looks in a single volume like digression could, at the pace of first reading, serve as the texture of a world one returned to.

Serial publication also made the novel unusually public while it was still unfinished. An instalment could be discussed before the next was written, and authors could, and sometimes did, adjust their plans in light of how readers responded. A purist may regard this as a surrender of artistic control. It can as easily be seen as a kind of conversation, in which a book took shape partly through its encounter with an audience rather than being delivered complete to readers whose only role was to receive it. For a time the novel became a shared event, followed by readers who knew that a great many others were reading the same pages in the same weeks.

None of this is to deny the costs. Writing to fixed lengths did produce padding, and the demand for suspense at the end of each part did produce cliffhangers that the story did not need and that read awkwardly once the parts were bound together. A reader of the collected volume is entitled to notice these. What deserves suspicion is the standard against which they are judged as failures: the ideal of the novel as a single, unified design, conceived whole and executed according to plan. That ideal has a history of its own, and it gained much of its authority later, among writers and critics who worked for a different market and under different conditions. Applied backward, it does not so much reveal the faults of serial fiction as convert its differences into faults.

There is, finally, an irony in the critical embarrassment. Long television dramas, released in episodes and discussed between them, are now often praised by critics for the very qualities, the slow accumulation, the large cast, the sense of a world lived alongside, that the instalment novel was once thought to need excusing for. Nothing about the newer form proves the older one right, and a slack episode is no better for belonging to a celebrated series. But the shift in judgement suggests that what critics of the instalment novel took for timeless standards of form were, at least in part, the habits of one particular way of reading.

CAT-VARC-P07-009 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Easy · 483 words · 4 questions

Few kinds of article travel as easily as the list of untranslatable words. A language is said to possess a single word for the particular light that falls through leaves, another for the guilty pleasure of buying books one will never read, a third for the melancholy of a Sunday evening, and the reader is invited to marvel that English, or whatever language the list happens to be written in, has nothing to match them. The lists are charming, and most of the words in them are real. What is doubtful is the conclusion they are usually taken to support: that the speakers of a language without such a word cannot quite have the experience it names, or that a vocabulary is a window onto a national soul.

Look at what the lists actually demonstrate. Each entry pairs a foreign word with a sentence or two of English explaining it, and the explanation is usually clear enough that the reader recognises the experience at once, often with a small shock of familiarity. The list, in other words, translates every word it calls untranslatable. What it shows is not that a meaning cannot cross from one language to another but that one language packages in a single word what another expresses in a phrase. That is a fact about how vocabularies happen to be divided up, and it tells us very little about what their speakers are able to think or feel. Every language lacks single words for countless things its speakers understand perfectly well.

This is not to say that nothing is lost when such a word is carried across. A single word is easy to reach for; a phrase is not. A word that ordinary speakers use daily carries the weight of all those uses, along with a register, a sound and a set of associations that no paraphrase reproduces. The very existence of the word suggests that a community has found the experience worth naming, and that fact is itself a small piece of cultural information. For a translator working on a poem, where sound and compression matter as much as sense, these losses are real and constant, and no amount of explanation in a footnote restores them.

But the losses are of degree, not of kind, and they are not peculiar to exotic words. They occur whenever any word is rendered by another, and even within a single language, where no two synonyms carry quite the same associations. Seen this way, the romance of the untranslatable flatters the reader, who feels worldly for knowing the rare word, and it encourages a picture of cultures as sealed rooms whose inhabitants can only be gazed at, never understood. The more modest and more accurate lesson is that translation is always possible and never complete, and that its difficulties lie less in the dictionary than in everything a word drags along with it.

Common misconception: Choosing a criticism that sounds reasonable in general but is not the one the passage makes.

CAT-VARC-P07-010 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Easy · 445 words · 4 questions

Whenever a much-loved novel reaches the screen, a familiar verdict follows: the book was better. Behind the verdict there usually lies a checklist. Was the heroine's age changed, a subplot dropped, a minor character merged with another, the ending softened? Each departure is counted as a fault, and the best adaptation, on this view, is simply the one that departs least, as though the novel were a fixed list of facts and the film an attempt, more or less competent, to copy that list onto a different surface. The habit is understandable, since readers who love a book feel protective of it, but as a standard for judging films it rests on a confusion about what an adaptation is.

A novel and a film do not tell stories in the same way. A novel can spend twenty pages inside a character's head; a film must find something for the camera to look at. A novel handles time loosely, lingering or summarising as it likes, while a film runs at a pace the viewer cannot slow. To reproduce a book's events faithfully is therefore no guarantee of reproducing what the book does. Imagine a novel narrated by a man whose account of himself the reader slowly learns to distrust. A film that simply stages the events he describes, shot plainly and from the outside, may keep every incident and still lose the book entirely, because the book was never really about the incidents but about the gap between what the narrator says and what the reader comes to see.

Conversely, an adaptation that alters a good deal may be truer to its source. A director who invents a visual device to unsettle our trust in what we are shown, framing scenes so that the viewer begins to doubt them, has understood that novel better than one who preserves its plot to the letter. The right question is not how much was kept but whether the film has found its own means of doing what the book did.

None of this means that every liberty is defensible. Some adaptations borrow a famous title for its prestige and its ready-made audience while discarding everything that made the book worth adapting, and the complaint against them is fair. But it is fair for a different reason than the checklist suggests. Such films fail not because they changed things but because they changed the wrong things, keeping the label and throwing away the substance. Fidelity, properly understood, is a matter of purpose rather than of inventory, and judging it requires the critic to say what a book was for before complaining about what a film has left out.

Paragraph 1 rejects the checklist standard, paragraphs 2 and 3 argue that a film must find 'its own means of doing what the book did', and paragraph 4 restates fidelity as 'a matter of purpose rather than of inventory'.

CAT-VARC-P07-011 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Medium · 462 words · 4 questions

A respectable argument holds that negative criticism of new books is a waste of everyone's time. So many books are published each year that no critic can notice more than a sliver of them; the space a journal devotes to criticism is therefore precious, and to spend it explaining why a book is bad is to deny it to a book that deserves attention. On this view the most damning verdict a critic can deliver is silence. A poor book left unmentioned simply sinks, and nothing is lost except the pleasure that some critics take in wounding. The position has the further appeal of seeming kind, which is no small recommendation in a literary world where writers and critics are often the same people.

The argument is tidy, but it quietly assumes that the only job of criticism is to guide readers in choosing what to read. If that were so, the notice of a weak book would indeed be wasted, since a reader who is told merely to skip something has learned nothing that he could use elsewhere. But criticism has always done something besides sorting. A careful account of why a book fails is also an account of what success would have looked like, and in giving it the critic makes public a set of standards that readers can test, dispute and apply for themselves. The reader who follows a well-argued case against a novel he admired may end unconvinced, yet he will read the next novel more alertly.

There is a further cost to silence. If critics write only about the books they admire, praise becomes the sole currency of criticism, and like any currency issued without limit it loses its value. A notice that can only ever say yes tells the reader very little, because its enthusiasm is not the outcome of a judgement that could have gone the other way. Readers soon learn to discount it, as they discount the admiring phrases printed on the cover of every new book, and the critic whose praise is sincere is then heard no more clearly than the publicist. The possibility of dispraise is what gives praise its meaning.

None of this licenses cruelty, and the case for restraint does have force in one kind of instance. A modest first novel from a small press, which few people will encounter anyway, gains nothing from a public demolition, and neither do its readers. The negative notice earns its space when the book in question is prominent: widely praised, heavily promoted, or likely to shape what others write. There the critic who dissents is not kicking someone who is down but contesting a reputation that is already shaping public taste, and that is among the most useful things criticism can do.

CAT-VARC-P07-011-Q3 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
According to the author, the argument for silence rests on which of the following assumptions?

  2. (B)Most of the books published each year are of poor quality and deserve no attention.

  4. (D)Critics take more pleasure in writing unfavourable notices than favourable ones.

Why not (D): Paragraph 1 mentions that 'some critics' enjoy wounding as the only thing lost by silence; it is not something the argument depends on.

Common misconception: Choosing a premise that sounds congenial to the argument but that the author does not identify.

Difficulty: The assumption is named by the author, but wrong options offer premises the argument might seem to need. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-011-Q4 · Except / not supported · DifficultMCQ

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 grants that restraint 'does have force' for a modest first novel 'which few people will encounter anyway'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says such an account 'is also an account of what success would have looked like', so the author agrees.

Common misconception: Taking careful argument as enough to rescue praise, and missing that paragraph 3 locates the problem in the impossibility of dispraise.

Difficulty: The correct answer sounds moderate and reasonable; rejecting it requires applying the reason given in paragraph 3 rather than its surface wording. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-012 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Medium · 428 words · 4 questions

Arguments about the literary canon are usually conducted as arguments about a list. One side defends a set of books as the best that a tradition has produced; the other replies that the list was drawn up by a narrow group of readers and reflects their tastes and their blind spots. Both sides, for all their hostility, agree on what a canon is: a ranking, a roll of honour, a verdict on quality that can be revised by adding names or striking them out. It is worth asking whether that shared assumption is right.

A different account fits the actual history of canons better. On this account a work becomes canonical not because readers judge it excellent but because later writers cannot leave it alone. They answer it, parody it, borrow its situations, quarrel with its conclusions and rewrite it from the point of view of a character it neglected. The canonical work is then the one you must have read in order to understand a great many others; its status is less a medal than a position in a network of replies. This explains something the list model cannot. Canons contain works that almost everyone agrees are clumsy, uneven or dull in long stretches, and they exclude works of great finish that simply provoked no answer. A ranking by quality would not look like that; a record of a conversation would.

The account has a consequence for those who wish to change the canon. If canonical status is conferred by later writing rather than by verdict, then a syllabus committee cannot make a book canonical by placing it on a reading list, any more than a critic could remove one by declaring it overrated. What can be done is to put neglected works where future writers will meet them, in print, in translation and in the classroom, and then to wait and see whether they are taken up.

Yet the conversational canon is not thereby cleared of the exclusions its critics describe. Who gets to join a conversation depends on who was taught to read, who could find a publisher and whose replies were preserved. A tradition in which most writers came from one class or one sex will have conducted its conversation largely among them, and the canon it produced will reflect that. The conversational account does not answer the critics of the canon; it relocates their complaint, from the judgements of tastemakers to the conditions under which people could write at all, and that is where the remedy, if there is one, must be sought.

CAT-VARC-P07-012-Q1 · Meaning in context · EasyMCQ
In paragraph 2, the statement that a canonical work's status is 'less a medal than a position in a network of replies' means that the work

  2. (B)has a reputation that rises and falls as each new generation of critics comments upon it.
  3. (C)is valued chiefly for its own replies to earlier books rather than for any originality of its own.
  4. (D)matters because so many later works respond to it, not because it has been judged to excel.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The surrounding sentences say a work becomes canonical 'because later writers cannot leave it alone' and is 'the one you must have read in order to understand a great many others'. A medal is a verdict of excellence; a position in a network is defined by the replies the work receives.

Why not (B): The replies are by 'later writers' who rewrite and parody the work, not by critics, and no rise and fall is described.

Why not (C): This reverses the direction: paragraph 2 describes later writers replying to the canonical work, not the work replying to its predecessors.

Common misconception: Missing the direction of the replies, which run from later works to the canonical one.

Difficulty: The metaphor is explained by the sentences on either side of it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-013 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Medium · 457 words · 4 questions

Translators of the great books of the past often remark on a puzzle. The original of an old epic or a classical tragedy does not seem to age; readers return to it century after century and find it as alive as ever. Yet translations of the same work date quickly. A version that seemed fresh to its first readers can sound stiff or quaint within a lifetime, and every generation or two a new one appears, prefaced by the claim that its predecessors have become unreadable. Why should the copy age when the original, which is older and in many ways stranger, does not? The question is worth more attention than it usually receives, because the answer says something about what a translation is.

The usual answer is linguistic: a translation is written in the language of its own moment, with that moment's idioms and its notions of what sounds dignified and what sounds plain, and these change. The original, by contrast, has been absorbed into a tradition that reads it as a historical text and forgives its strangeness. There is truth in this, but it cannot be the whole story, since old translations are often perfectly intelligible and still feel inadequate.

A deeper reason lies in what translation requires. A great work is full of places where its sense is undecided: a word that could mean anger or grief, a line whose tone could be mocking or tender, a scene whose moral weight is left to the reader. The original can leave such places open, and much of its power to sustain rereading comes from that openness; a translator has no such freedom, because writing in another language forces choices at every point, and each choice settles a question the original left unsettled. The choices are not arbitrary, but they are shaped by what the translator's own age finds plausible or important, and so the translation records one generation's reading of the work. When the concerns of that generation fade, its translation comes to look like what it always was: an interpretation.

Seen in this light, retranslation is not a sign that earlier translators failed. It is one of the ways a classic stays alive, each version making a fresh set of decisions and so showing a new reader something the others concealed. There is even an occasional reversal. Some translations become works of literature in their own right, read not as windows onto the original but for their own sake, and these, curiously, stop ageing in the way translations usually do; they come to be read, like originals, as historical texts. Their choices no longer look like a period's reading of another book; they look like the permanent features of a book of their own.

CAT-VARC-P07-014 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Difficult · 460 words · 4 questions

Few questions in criticism have been argued so long, or with so little sign of settlement, as whether the meaning of a work is what its author intended. The intentionalist says yes: a text is an utterance, utterances are made by someone for a purpose, and to ignore that purpose is to read not the work but a sequence of marks that happens to resemble it. The anti-intentionalist says no: the author's state of mind is usually unavailable, frequently confused and in any case beside the point, since a published work belongs to the public language in which it was written, and its meaning is whatever that language, competently read, makes of it.

Each side wins its arguments by making the other's view look absurd, and each is right about what it denies. The intentionalist is right that we cannot read without attributing purposes. To notice that a sentence is ironic, that a repetition is deliberate or that a sudden plainness of style marks a turning point is already to treat the text as the product of choices; strip away every assumption about purpose and the irony vanishes along with the design. The anti-intentionalist is right that a letter to a newspaper or a diary entry in which the author explains what the book means has no special authority over the book. Authors misremember, revise their motives after the fact and sometimes simply misjudge what they have made.

The mistake common to both is to picture intention as a private event lying behind the text, which the text either reports faithfully or conceals. Intention in writing is better understood the way we understand it in action. We do not usually discover what someone meant by watching them and then consulting their inner life; we read the purpose in the shape of what they did. In the same way the intentions that matter to criticism are those embodied in a work's choices, its emphases and omissions, the expectations it sets up and then disappoints. They are public because the work is.

This view does not make the author irrelevant, nor does it make the author final. A writer's notebooks may help us see a pattern we had missed, as the testimony of a witness may help a court, but the evidence is then the pattern, not the testimony. And a work may carry implications its author never foresaw and would perhaps have disowned: a novel written to celebrate a way of life can, through the very details it lovingly records, reveal the costs that way of life imposed on those at its margins. To point this out is not to defy the author's intention but to take the work's design more seriously than its author's later account of it.

CAT-VARC-P07-015 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Medium · 604 words · 5 questions

It is often said that a book can be read for the first time only once, and the remark is usually meant as a lament. On a first reading we do not know what will happen; we are carried by suspense, surprised by turns we did not foresee, and moved in a way that, the argument goes, can never quite be recovered. Rereading, on this view, is a kind of nostalgia: a return to a place whose excitement has been used up, pleasant perhaps, but a lesser thing than the original encounter. Schools encourage the same view without meaning to, since a syllabus moves on once a book has been 'done', as though a single passage through it were the whole of the work. The lament has real feeling behind it, and anyone who remembers staying up late to finish a novel knows what it describes.

There is a case for the opposite view, that in an important sense only rereading is reading. A first reading is dominated by one question, what happens next, and that question consumes most of our attention. We skim descriptions to reach events, miss the significance of details whose importance has not yet been revealed and form judgements of characters that the book later overturns. Only when we know the ending can we attend to how the book reaches it: the early scene that quietly prepares a later one, the image that returns with altered meaning, the minor character whose remark, seemingly idle at first, turns out to state the novel's theme. Knowing the outcome does not empty these details; it fills them. Consider a novel built around a concealed secret. Read a second time, its hints, which at first seemed part of the scenery, stand out as hints, and the reader can admire the skill with which they were hidden in plain sight, a pleasure that the first reader, hurrying towards the revelation, could not have had.

Rereading also reveals a change in the reader. The novel read at fifteen and again at forty has not altered a word, yet it is a different book, because the person reading it has acquired experiences that let different passages speak. A story of a marriage that seemed, to the young reader, a tale of dull people becomes, to the older one, almost unbearably exact. Nothing on the page has moved; what has moved is the range of experience the reader can bring to it, and with it the passages that seem to matter. In this sense a book reread is a kind of instrument for measuring oneself, and the measurements are often humbling: we discover what we were once unable to see, and sometimes that the passages we admired most were the weakest.

None of this means that the first reading is worthless. It has a quality nothing later can supply, an innocence about what is coming that the writer counted on, and some effects are designed for it alone: the twist that recasts everything before it can surprise only once. Moreover, without a first reading there is nothing to reread, and the second encounter depends on the first even when it corrects it. Some books, indeed, are exhausted by one passage through them, and this is perhaps the most useful test of all. A book that offers nothing on a second reading was a mechanism for producing suspense, admirable in its way, while a book that gives more each time was something else. The difference between the two kinds is not visible from inside a first reading, which is one more reason to distrust the verdicts we reach there.

  4. (D)noticing images that return later in the book with an altered meaning.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says that on a first reading 'We skim descriptions to reach events'.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 lists returning images among what we can attend to 'Only when we know the ending'.

Common misconception: Picking a rereading experience because it appears in the same list.

Difficulty: Stated in one sentence of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-015-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ

  2. (B)such details gain significance once a reader can see what they were preparing for.
  3. (C)a reader who knows the ending will find such details tedious, though still necessary to the book.
  4. (D)the suspense of a first reading is replaced on rereading by a complete knowledge of the plot's events.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

The 'details' meant are the preparing scene, the returning image and the idle remark that 'turns out to state the novel's theme'. Their importance 'has not yet been revealed' on a first reading; knowing the ending reveals it, so the details are filled with meaning.

Why not (A): Nothing in the passage concerns readers inventing material; the details meant are those already in the text.

Why not (C): This is close to the opposite: the sentence denies that knowledge of the outcome empties the details.

Why not (D): The rereader does know the events, but the sentence is about what that knowledge does to 'these details', namely make them meaningful.

Common misconception: Choosing an option that is true of rereading in general but is not what the sentence says about the details.

Difficulty: The metaphor must be read against the list it concludes; one wrong option states a true but different point. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-015-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The example of the story of a marriage (paragraph 3) suggests that
  1. (A)a reader's growing experience can reveal qualities in a book that were present but unseen before.
  2. (B)young readers are usually right to find stories about ordinary people rather dull.
  3. (C)novels about marriage are best understood by readers who are themselves married.

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3 says the reader 'has acquired experiences that let different passages speak', and the marriage story becomes 'almost unbearably exact'. The exactness was there; the young reader lacked the experience to see it.

Why not (B): The older reader finds the story 'almost unbearably exact', so the passage implies the young reader missed something.

Why not (C): The example contrasts a younger and an older reader, not married and unmarried ones; this generalises beyond the passage.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the book 'has not altered a word' and locates the change in 'the person reading it', not in the culture.
