CAT-VARC-P07-015-Q5 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following would the author most likely regard as the best evidence that a novel is more than 'a mechanism for producing suspense'?
  1. (A)Readers who begin it find themselves unable to put it down until they have reached the last page.
  2. (B)Critics praised it highly on its first appearance, well before its ending had become widely known.
  3. (C)Readers who already know how it ends report finding more in it on a third reading than on a second.
  4. (D)Its final twist recasts the earlier chapters so completely that first-time readers are astonished by it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 4 makes the test whether a book 'offers nothing on a second reading' or 'gives more each time'. Only the correct answer reports what readers find after the ending is known, and it shows the book giving more each time.

Why not (A): Compulsive reading is what suspense produces; it is a first-reading effect and is what a mere mechanism would also achieve.

Why not (B): These are verdicts reached 'from inside a first reading', which the author says we should distrust.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says such a twist 'can surprise only once'; it is an effect designed for the first reading and says nothing about later ones.

Common misconception: Choosing the twist because recasting earlier chapters sounds like what rereading does, though it happens on the first reading.

Difficulty: The test must be applied precisely; the twist wrong option borrows the language of rereading. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-016 · Reading, translation and criticism · passage Difficult · 586 words · 5 questions

Literary history has always been written from a remarkably small sample. Of the many thousands of novels published in a given century, a scholar can read a few hundred in a working life, and the histories of the form are built on a smaller set still, the books that later readers chose to keep in print. When such a history says that 'the novel' turned inward at a certain period, or abandoned the omniscient narrator, or grew preoccupied with the city, it is generalising from a selection that was never meant to be representative. Nobody chose the canon as a sample; it was assembled by generations of readers for reasons of taste, teaching and accident, and there is no reason to expect it to resemble the whole from which it was drawn. The advocates of computational or 'distant' reading start from this embarrassment. If machines can process an entire century's output, counting words, tracking the length of sentences or the frequency with which characters are named, then claims about the form as a whole can at last be tested against the whole.

The objections are familiar, and some are serious. Counting is not reading. The meaning of a word depends on its context, on irony, on the dramatic situation in which it is spoken, none of which a frequency table records. Critics add that many of the patterns such studies report are either trivial or confirm what attentive readers already knew, so that a great deal of machinery produces a modest result dressed in charts. There is also a subtler worry: the features a computer can count are those that can be defined mechanically, and these are not obviously the features that matter. A discipline that adopts such tools risks drifting towards studying what can be measured simply because it can be measured. Defenders of the method reply that every discipline begins by measuring crude things, but the reply concedes more than it denies.

Yet much of this debate rests on a false picture of the choice. Distant reading does not replace close reading so much as change what close reading is asked to do. Its most interesting results are rarely the patterns themselves; they are the anomalies, the decade in which a measure behaves unexpectedly or the obscure novels that cluster with canonical ones on some feature nobody had thought to examine. A measure that shows some habit of style rising steadily for decades and then collapsing within a few years explains nothing by itself, but it tells a scholar exactly where to look. Such a finding means little until someone goes back and reads the books in question, and it is that return which produces understanding. The count poses a question; it does not answer one.

Where the method does make an independent contribution is in the matter of the sample. Even if a computer can say little about what any single novel means, it can show that a generalisation drawn from the canon fails to hold for the much larger body of books that the canon left out. That is not a trivial service. A claim about what 'the novel' did, which turns out to describe only the few hundred books that survived into the syllabus, was never a claim about the novel at all, but about the tastes of the readers who did the selecting. The modest promise of distant reading, then, is less to tell us what literature means than to tell us more accurately which literature our claims are about.

CAT-VARC-P07-016-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, which of the following is an objection that has been made to distant reading?
  1. (A)The charts that such studies produce are too complex for most literary scholars to interpret.
  2. (B)Computers are not yet able to process anything close to the full output of a century of novels.
  3. (C)Distant reading has been applied mostly to canonical novels and has neglected forgotten ones.
  4. (D)A frequency count cannot register the irony or dramatic context on which a word's meaning depends.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 2: 'The meaning of a word depends on its context, on irony, on the dramatic situation in which it is spoken, none of which a frequency table records.'

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 complains of 'a modest result dressed in charts', a point about the modesty of results, not the difficulty of charts.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 presents processing a century's output as what the method makes possible; no one in the passage objects that it cannot be done.

Why not (C): The passage never says this; paragraph 4 credits the method with reaching 'the much larger body of books that the canon left out'.

Common misconception: Choosing an option that echoes the word 'charts' or 'century' from the passage.

Difficulty: The objection is stated directly in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-016-Q2 · Inference · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P07-017 · Visual art and museums · passage Easy · 467 words · 4 questions

Few things in a museum are read as often, or looked at as little, as the wall label. Visitors approaching a painting frequently read the small panel beside it first, glance at the picture to confirm what they have been told, and move on. Curators have noticed this pattern for a long time, and it has produced two opposite reactions. One camp argues that labels should be shortened or removed altogether, so that the visitor is thrown back onto the object. The other argues that labels should be expanded, written in friendlier language and supplemented with audio guides and screens, since a public that does not know what it is looking at cannot be expected to care.

Both reactions treat the label as though its only variable were quantity. The purist assumes that less text means more looking, but a visitor confronted by an unexplained altarpiece does not usually begin to look harder; more often she feels excluded and walks faster. The expansionist assumes that more text means more understanding, yet a long label that rehearses the artist's biography, the patron's family and the painting's later owners gives the visitor a great deal to know and very little to see. In each case the label is imagined as a container of facts, and the only argument is over how full the container should be.

A better question is what the label asks the visitor to do. A label can close a picture, by telling the viewer what it means, or it can open it, by directing attention to something that can be checked against the canvas itself. Compare two sentences about the same imagined portrait. The first says that the sitter was a wealthy merchant who commissioned the work to celebrate his marriage. The second says that the merchant's left hand rests on a ledger while his right holds his wife's glove, and invites the reader to decide which hand the painter has lit more brightly. The first sentence can be absorbed without raising one's eyes; the second cannot be finished without looking.

None of this means that historical information is a distraction. A viewer who does not know that a certain blue, ground from a semi-precious stone, was once among the costliest materials a painter could buy will miss part of what a patron was displaying when he asked for a sky full of it, and no amount of attentive looking will supply that fact. The point is that information earns its place on the wall when it changes what the visitor notices. Facts that merely sit beside the object, however accurate, belong in the catalogue, where they can be read at leisure by those who want them. The wall is too valuable, and the visitor's attention too brief, to be spent on anything else.

CAT-VARC-P07-017-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)show that visitors who read labels before looking at a painting understand it less well than those who do not.
  2. (B)propose that a label should be judged by whether it sends the visitor back to the work, not by its length.
  3. (C)argue that museums should replace long wall labels with audio guides and screens that visitors can consult at will.
  4. (D)defend the purist view that a painting should be encountered without any written explanation beside it.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 says both camps wrongly treat quantity as the label's 'only variable'; paragraph 3 replaces that with the question of 'what the label asks the visitor to do'; paragraph 4 concludes that information belongs on the wall 'when it changes what the visitor notices'. The purpose is to reframe how labels should be judged.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 describes the habit of reading first and glancing after, but the passage never compares the understanding of two groups of visitors; the claim invents a finding.

Why not (C): Audio guides and screens appear in paragraph 1 only as part of the expansionist camp's proposal, which the author goes on to criticise in paragraph 2; the author recommends nothing of the kind.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 rejects the purist assumption, saying an unexplained altarpiece makes a visitor feel 'excluded', and paragraph 4 keeps a place for historical information on the wall.

Common misconception: Taking one camp described in paragraph 1 as the author's own position.

Difficulty: The thesis is stated outright in paragraphs 3 and 4 and the two camps are clearly marked as views the author rejects. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

The paragraph introduces the contrast between labels that 'close' and labels that 'open' a picture, and the closing sentence makes the point of the example: 'The first sentence can be absorbed without raising one's eyes; the second cannot be finished without looking.'

CAT-VARC-P07-017-Q4 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P07-018 · Visual art and museums · passage Easy · 429 words · 4 questions

When a town decides to honour someone or something in public, its first instinct is usually to reach for bronze and stone. Durability is treated as a measure of respect: a statue meant to last for centuries seems to say more than a banner meant to last for a week. Yet the history of public art suggests that permanence is less a virtue than a wager, and frequently a losing one. A monument fixes one generation's judgement in a material that will outlast the judgement itself. Within a few decades the figure on the plinth may be unrecognised by most passers-by, and within a century it may be the cause of a quarrel its makers could not have imagined.

The problem is not that later generations are fickle. It is that a permanent work is designed to end discussion rather than to take part in it. Once installed, it asks only to be accepted; there is no procedure built into it for being reconsidered. When disagreement finally arrives, it therefore arrives as a crisis, with petitions, counter-petitions and arguments over whether removal amounts to erasing history. A city that had expected its public art to change would face no such crisis, because it would already possess a habit of revisiting what stands in its squares.

Temporary works offer one way of building that habit. A plinth that hosts a different commission every year or two, a sculpture placed in a park for a single season, a mural painted on the understanding that it will be painted over: each of these makes public art an occasion rather than a verdict. People argue about the new piece, grow fond of it or feel relieved to see it go, and in doing so they practise exactly the kind of collective judgement that a permanent statue forecloses. The work matters less as an object than as the conversation it starts.

This argument has limits, and they should be stated. Temporary art can slide into novelty for its own sake, a rotating series of spectacles chosen to attract visitors rather than to say anything. And some public works aim at permanence for good reason: a memorial to the dead of a war or a disaster is meant to resist the passing of time, because forgetting is precisely the danger it guards against. The case against bronze is not a case against all lasting memorials. It is a case against the assumption that whatever a public wishes to honour must be honoured in a form that can never be questioned without a fight.

CAT-VARC-P07-018-Q1 · Inference · MediumMCQ

  1. (A)the statues offer no built-in occasion for reconsideration, so disagreement is held back until it bursts out all at once.
  2. (B)bronze and stone are expensive materials, and removing works made of them wastes the money a town once spent.
  3. (C)the statues are usually so unfamiliar to passers-by that few people are able to say why they were put up.
  4. (D)later generations change their opinions too quickly to respect the judgements of the people who raised the statues.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 2 says a permanent work 'asks only to be accepted' with 'no procedure built into it for being reconsidered', and so 'When disagreement finally arrives, it therefore arrives as a crisis'.

Why not (B): Cost is never raised; the passage treats durable materials as a statement of respect and of permanence, not as an expense to be protected.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says many such figures become unrecognised, but paragraph 2 locates the cause of the crisis in the absence of any 'procedure' for reconsideration, not in ignorance.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 opens by denying exactly this: 'The problem is not that later generations are fickle.'

Common misconception: Picking the explanation the author explicitly rules out (fickleness), or taking a detail from paragraph 1 as the cause.

Difficulty: The correct answer must be reconstructed from the causal chain in paragraph 2, and one wrong option reuses a real detail from paragraph 1. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-018-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · MediumMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's claim that a city expecting its public art to change would face no crisis over what stands in its squares?
  1. (A)Several temporary sculptures have provoked angry letters to newspapers and heated public meetings while they were on display.
  2. (B)Visitors to war memorials often say that the permanence of the stone is what makes the place feel solemn to them.
  3. (C)A city with a rotating plinth for decades still endured years of bitter petitions over removing a nearby statue.
  4. (D)Most of the works shown on rotating plinths are forgotten by the public within a year or two of being removed.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2 claims that a city used to change 'would already possess a habit of revisiting' and so would avoid crisis. A city with exactly that habit that still suffered a prolonged crisis is a direct counterexample.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 welcomes argument about the new piece; lively debate over a temporary work is the habit the author wants, not the crisis he warns against.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 already grants that memorials to the dead aim at permanence for good reason, so this agrees with the author rather than weakening him.

Why not (D): Forgetting a temporary work after it leaves is compatible with the author's view that such works matter as the 'conversation' they start, so it does not touch the claim.

Common misconception: Treating any public argument as the 'crisis' the author predicts, when he welcomes argument about temporary works.

Difficulty: The solver must separate the debate the author welcomes from the crisis he says the habit prevents. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-019 · Visual art and museums · passage Medium · 411 words · 4 questions

Arguments about the cleaning of old paintings tend to repeat themselves with remarkable fidelity. A famous canvas is cleaned; the removal of darkened varnish reveals colours far brighter than anyone living has seen on it; and two parties form at once. One celebrates the recovery of what the painter actually intended, a picture freed from centuries of grime. The other mourns the loss of a mellow harmony that generations had loved, and suspects that the cleaners, in their zeal, have removed not only dirt but the delicate final glazes with which the painter softened his effects.

It is tempting to treat this as a contest between truth and sentiment, with the cleaners on the side of truth. There is something to this. Natural resin varnishes do yellow and darken with age, and a sky that now looks olive may well have been painted as a clear blue; to prefer the olive because one is used to it is to prefer an accident over a decision. Cleaning has repeatedly shown that painters long thought murky were in fact bold colourists, and no honest account of the debate can pretend otherwise.

Yet the cleaners' strongest claim, that they return the painting to its original state, is harder to sustain than it sounds. Pigments themselves change: some blues fade, some greens turn brown, some reds lose their glazes to light long before any restorer arrives. Removing the varnish from such a picture does not take it back to the day it left the studio; it produces a new object, one in which unchanged passages and altered ones sit side by side in proportions the painter never saw. The bright painting revealed by cleaning may therefore be no closer to the original than the dark one it replaced, only differently distant from it.

What follows is not that cleaning should stop, but that it should be described honestly. Every restoration is a judgement about which of a painting's many histories to favour, and judgements can be wrong. The responsible course is to make such judgements in ways that can be undone where possible, to record exactly what has been removed and why, and to abandon the language of revelation in favour of the language of interpretation. A restorer who says 'this is what the painter meant' claims more than any evidence can give; one who says 'this is our best reading, and here is how we reached it' claims only what the craft can support.

CAT-VARC-P07-019-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
Which point in favour of the cleaners does the author accept?
  1. (A)Varnish yellows with age, so preferring the darkened colours can mean preferring an accident to the painter's choice.
  2. (B)Cleaned paintings are in every respect identical to the pictures as they looked when they left the studio.
  3. (C)Cleaners have never removed the final glazes that painters used to soften their effects.
  4. (D)Admirers of the darkened pictures are moved by sentiment and have no real reason for their preference.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 says 'Natural resin varnishes do yellow and darken with age' and adds that 'to prefer the olive because one is used to it is to prefer an accident over a decision.'

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 denies this directly: cleaning 'does not take it back to the day it left the studio' because pigments have also changed.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 reports the mourners' suspicion that cleaners have removed final glazes, and the author never dismisses it, so the passage gives no such guarantee.

Why not (D): The author reports the 'truth and sentiment' framing as 'tempting' and grants only part of it; he never says the mourners have no reason, and paragraph 1 records their worry about lost glazes.

Common misconception: Stretching the author's partial concession into full agreement with the cleaners.

Difficulty: The concession is stated in one place and introduced by 'There is something to this.' · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-019-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
The argument in paragraph 3 that cleaning cannot return a painting to its original state relies on which of the following assumptions?
  1. (A)The varnish on old paintings was not applied by the painters themselves as part of the finished work.
  2. (B)Removing varnish does not also undo the fading and browning that the pigments themselves have undergone.
  3. (C)Viewers who knew the painting before cleaning were mistaken about the colours the painter had chosen.
  4. (D)The darkened state of a painting before cleaning was closer to its original appearance than the cleaned state.

Why not (C): Earlier viewers' beliefs play no role in paragraph 3; the argument concerns physical change in the picture, not anyone's opinion of it.

Why not (D): The author says the two are 'differently distant' from the original and needs no ranking between them; asserting this would actually go beyond his argument.

Common misconception: Choosing a claim that is related to the debate (who applied the varnish, which state is closer) but that the argument does not need.

Difficulty: Requires identifying a necessary, unstated link and rejecting two options that strengthen or extend the case without being required. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-020 · Visual art and museums · passage Medium · 412 words · 4 questions

Discussion of whether photographs can be trusted has long been dominated by the fear of manipulation. Early critics worried about staged scenes and retouched negatives; later ones about software that can add a figure to a crowd or remove one from a podium without leaving a trace visible to the eye. These worries are reasonable, and the tools that prompt them have become more powerful. But they direct attention to the least interesting way in which a photograph can mislead, and in doing so they leave the more ordinary and more pervasive problem almost untouched.

Consider an unaltered photograph of a crowded square taken during a demonstration. Every figure in it was really there, standing exactly where the picture shows. The image is, in the narrow sense, entirely truthful. Yet it cannot tell us whether the square was full or whether the photographer stood at the one corner where people had gathered; whether the moment captured was typical of the afternoon or its single tense minute; whether the raised fists at the front belonged to protesters or to the counter-demonstration across the road. A photograph is accurate about what falls inside its frame and wholly silent about what falls outside it, and the frame is chosen by someone.

This is why the manipulation debate can produce a false sense of security. A viewer who has learned to check for doctored pixels may conclude that an image which passes the check is reliable, when the check has said nothing about selection, timing or framing. An honest photograph can support a dishonest story as easily as a forged one can, and often more easily, since it will survive the very scrutiny that exposes a forgery.

None of this makes photographs worthless as evidence. It means that their evidential value lies less in the single image than in its surroundings: the caption that states when and where it was taken, the other frames from the same roll or memory card, the identity and record of the person who took it, the existence of independent pictures of the same event. A photograph considered alone is a fragment that can be fitted into many stories. A photograph considered with these supports begins to narrow the stories it can serve. The question to ask of an image, then, is not only whether it has been altered but what would have to be true of everything around it for it to mean what it is said to mean.

CAT-VARC-P07-020-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best expresses the main point of the passage?
  1. (A)Viewers should learn to check images for doctored pixels, because unaltered photographs are always accurate about the events they record.
  2. (B)Concern with doctored images neglects the commoner danger of selective framing, so a photograph's worth as evidence rests on its context.
  3. (C)Photographers who choose where to stand and when to press the shutter are mainly responsible for the dishonest stories told with images.
  4. (D)Software that can alter photographs invisibly has made every image unreliable, so photographs should no longer count as evidence.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 says the manipulation worry leaves 'the more ordinary and more pervasive problem almost untouched'; paragraphs 2 and 3 identify that problem as framing and selection; paragraph 4 locates evidential value 'in its surroundings'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 warns that passing this check gives a 'false sense of security', and paragraph 2 says an unaltered image can mislead about the event.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 notes that 'the frame is chosen by someone', but the passage never blames photographers; its point is about how viewers should weigh images.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says 'None of this makes photographs worthless as evidence', and paragraph 1 calls manipulation 'the least interesting way' a photograph misleads.

Common misconception: Fixing on the manipulation theme of paragraph 1 or on the photographer's choice in paragraph 2.

Difficulty: The correct answer must combine the critique and the positive conclusion; two wrong options exaggerate claims the passage actually makes. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-021 · Visual art and museums · passage Medium · 411 words · 4 questions

When a painting sells at auction for a sum that would fund a hospital, two responses compete for the headlines. The first treats the price as a verdict: the market, pooling the judgements of many informed buyers, has declared this one of the most important works of its kind. The second treats it as a scandal: the sum has nothing to do with art and everything to do with vanity, speculation and the search for places to park wealth. Each response captures something, but both share a mistaken premise, which is that the price is either a measure of artistic value or a measure of nothing at all.

A price measures what someone will pay to own a thing, and owning a painting is not the same as valuing it as a painting. The buyer acquires an object that is unique, that cannot be reproduced without losing the very property being purchased, and that carries a name capable of conferring distinction on whoever holds it. Each of these features has a market logic of its own. Scarcity helps explain why a minor work by a celebrated painter can outsell a masterpiece by an obscure one; the name explains why a newly accepted attribution can multiply a canvas's price overnight without a single brushstroke changing. In neither case has anyone's experience of looking at the picture altered.

This does not mean that prices float free of critical judgement. Over long periods, the artists whom critics, historians and museums take seriously tend also to be the ones whose works command high prices, and it would be perverse to call this coincidence. But the connection runs largely through reputation rather than through the works themselves: esteem builds a name, the name creates demand, and demand sets the price. The market thus registers critical judgement at second hand, much as a credit rating registers a company's prospects without being the same thing as its earnings.

The real danger lies in the return journey. Once prices are reported as verdicts, they begin to shape the judgements they supposedly reflect. Visitors crowd around the record-breaking canvas while the room beside it stands empty; institutions hesitate to show or lend works whose insurance values have become unmanageable; young artists learn which styles sell. A measure of ownership, mistaken for a measure of worth, ends by teaching people what to look at. Resisting that confusion requires nothing more radical than remembering what a price is the price of.

Why not (B): Museum competition is not given as the cause; paragraph 4 mentions institutions only as hesitating over works with unmanageable insurance values.

CAT-VARC-P07-022 · Visual art and museums · passage Difficult · 459 words · 4 questions

Suppose a painting has hung for decades in a museum, admired by critics and loved by visitors, and is then shown beyond doubt to be a forgery. Nothing about the canvas has changed: its colours and its composition are exactly as they were, and the pleasure that thousands took in it was genuine pleasure, not a misunderstanding awaiting correction. Yet the picture is taken down, its value collapses, and the critics who praised it are embarrassed. If what matters in a painting is what can be seen in it, this reaction looks irrational, a triumph of the label over the eye.

Two familiar explanations are offered, and neither is adequate. The first says the reaction is mere snobbery: we cared about the famous name, not the picture, and the forgery has simply exposed the fact. There is truth here, since much of the fall in price reflects nothing loftier than the loss of a signature that could be resold. But the explanation proves too much; it implies that anyone who minds about the difference is a snob, including the scrupulous historian with no money at stake. The second says that the forgery must differ visibly after all, and that trained eyes, once alerted, can see its deadness. Sometimes this is so; yet the deadness is usually perceived only after the verdict is known, and a theory that can detect a difference only once it has been told where to look is not much of a theory.

A more promising account begins from the thought that we value paintings not only as arrangements of colour but also as achievements. To admire a picture fully is partly to admire what was done in making it: a problem of light or space confronted at a particular moment, with the means then available, by someone who did not know how it would turn out. The forger faces a different problem, that of producing something that will pass as the solution to the first. The two canvases may look alike, but they answer different questions, and the exposure tells us that the question we thought we were admiring an answer to was never asked by this canvas at all.

This view has a cost, and it should be admitted. It makes part of a painting's value depend on facts that cannot be seen in it, and so concedes that looking alone can never be the whole of appreciation. It also allows that a forgery may be a considerable achievement of its own kind, as a feat of imitation, without ranking it beside what it imitates. But the cost seems worth paying, because the alternative is to hold that the history of art could be rewritten, without loss, by anyone with a sufficiently skilful hand.

CAT-VARC-P07-022-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ

  1. (A)cast doubt on the second explanation by pointing out that the supposed visible flaws are noticed only after exposure.
  2. (B)introduce the author's own account, which holds that forgeries can always be distinguished by close study.
  3. (C)reject the first explanation by showing that critics who praised the forgery were not motivated by snobbery.
  4. (D)support the second explanation by showing that trained eyes can find the deadness of a forgery once they are alerted.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2 presents the second explanation, that forgeries 'differ visibly after all', concedes 'Sometimes this is so', and then objects that the deadness 'is usually perceived only after the verdict is known'. The quoted remark completes that objection.

Why not (B): The author's account in paragraph 3 says the two canvases 'may look alike' and locates the difference in achievement, not in anything visible.

Why not (C): The remark concerns the claim of visible difference, the second explanation; the snobbery explanation is answered separately with the 'proves too much' objection.

Why not (D): The remark comes after 'yet' and undercuts the explanation; the author grants only that 'Sometimes this is so'.

Common misconception: Missing the 'yet' and reading the remark as support, or attaching it to the wrong explanation.

Difficulty: The remark's target is clear once the two explanations in paragraph 2 are kept apart. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-022-Q2 · Inference · DifficultMCQ

  2. (B)allow that someone could mind the difference for reasons other than prestige or resale.

CAT-VARC-P07-022-Q3 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following situations is most closely analogous to the author's own account of why the exposure of a forgery changes our appreciation?

  2. (B)A copy of a novel loses most of its value to collectors when it turns out to be a later printing, though its text is identical.
  3. (C)A chess game admired as brilliant play in an unforeseen position is found to have been copied move for move from an older game.
  4. (D)A wine praised in a blind tasting is revealed to be a cheap one, after which the same tasters begin to describe it as thin.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says we admire a work partly as 'a problem of light or space confronted at a particular moment' by someone 'who did not know how it would turn out', and that a forgery answers a different question. The copied chess game has identical moves, but the achievement admired, solving an unforeseen position, never took place.

Why not (A): This matches the concession in paragraph 4 that imitation can be 'a considerable achievement of its own kind', not the account of why exposure changes appreciation.

Why not (B): This mirrors the snobbery explanation: the loss reflects the market for a desirable label, the part the author grants but calls inadequate.

Why not (D): This mirrors the second explanation, where a flaw is 'perceived only after the verdict is known', which the author rejects.

Common misconception: Choosing the case that fits one of the explanations the author reports and rejects, or the concession in paragraph 4.

Difficulty: Every wrong option maps onto a real element of the passage, so the solver must match the specific structure of the author's own account. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-022-Q4 · Author's tone / attitude · DifficultMCQ
The author's attitude toward the view that 'what matters in a painting is what can be seen in it' is best described as
  1. (A)endorsing it reluctantly, since the reaction to forgery cannot be explained without some appeal to visible features.
  2. (B)dismissing it as a form of snobbery that values the label over the evidence of the eye.
  3. (C)accepting it for ordinary visitors while holding that experts must also weigh facts about a painting's making.
  4. (D)rejecting it as a full account, while admitting his alternative makes value rest partly on unseen facts.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 says that on this view the reaction 'looks irrational'; paragraph 3 replaces it with the idea of paintings as achievements; paragraph 4 admits the 'cost' that 'looking alone can never be the whole of appreciation' and judges it 'worth paying'.

Why not (B): The view prizes the eye, not the label: paragraph 1 says that on this view the reaction to forgery looks like 'a triumph of the label over the eye'. Snobbery is the label-loving attitude, so the option inverts the view.

Why not (C): The author draws no line between ordinary visitors and experts; he says 'looking alone can never be the whole of appreciation' for anyone.

CAT-VARC-P07-023 · Visual art and museums · passage Medium · 600 words · 5 questions

We tend to judge a portrait by asking whether it captures the person. A good portrait, on this view, reveals something of the sitter's inner life: a habit of mind, a private sorrow, the particular way one face differs from all others. Measured against this standard, many older portraits seem stiff and oddly impersonal. Rows of nobles stand in nearly identical poses, their faces composed into the same calm, their individuality apparently buried beneath velvet and armour. It is easy to conclude that their painters were trying to capture the person and largely failing.

That conclusion mistakes what such portraits were for. For much of the history of European portraiture, and of several other traditions, the purpose of a commissioned portrait was less to record a personality than to establish a position. The sitter appeared as a holder of office, an heir, a bride, the head of a house. Costume, setting and objects carried meanings that contemporaries could read: the chain of a civic office, a dog standing for fidelity, a column suggesting constancy, a distant estate glimpsed through a window. A portrait of this kind was closer to a document than to a confession. Its sameness was not a failure of observation but a deliberate placing of the individual within a recognisable type, since what the patron wished to have recorded was precisely the type he had come to occupy. A contemporary who knew the conventions could read an entire biography of rank in a single canvas, whereas a modern viewer expecting the revelation of a character finds only an inventory of possessions.

Likeness was not irrelevant. Families wanted to know what an absent bride looked like, and painters could be criticised for flattering too much or too little. But likeness served the larger purpose rather than defining it. A face had to be recognisable enough to attach the role to the right person, and beyond that the painter's task was to make the person fit the role. The expectation that a portrait should instead expose the sitter, catching him off guard or revealing what he might prefer to hide, belongs to a later and narrower moment in the genre's history. Where an older portrait does strike modern eyes as startlingly intimate, it is worth asking whether it was made for a public hall or for a private room, since the obligations of display weighed very differently in the two settings.

Several changes made that expectation possible. The growth of a buying public outside courts and great houses created sitters with no office to display. New ideas about the self encouraged the belief that a person's truth lay within rather than in his station. And when photography arrived in the nineteenth century, it soon took over much of the trade in cheap likenesses, leaving painters free, or obliged, to offer something a camera seemed unable to supply. The psychological portrait, in other words, is not the essence of the genre finally achieved but one of its historical forms.

To say this is not to claim that the older portraits had no inner life, or that modern ones are free of display. A shrewd painter could let a sitter's character show through the conventions, and a present-day portrait of a company chairman, with its carefully chosen office and posture, documents a position as surely as any court painting did. The point is only that we should judge a portrait by the question it was asked to answer. Faulting a court painter for failing to reveal a soul is like faulting a map for failing to be a landscape painting.

CAT-VARC-P07-023-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)arguing that older portraits are artistically superior to modern ones because they honestly record a sitter's social position.
  2. (B)explaining how photography destroyed the market for painted portraits in the nineteenth century and after.
  3. (C)describing the symbols, such as dogs and columns, that court painters used to signal their sitters' virtues.
  4. (D)showing that the demand for psychological insight in portraits is a historical development, not a timeless standard for the genre.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 states the modern standard; paragraphs 2 and 3 show older portraits served a different purpose; paragraph 4 says the psychological portrait 'is not the essence of the genre finally achieved but one of its historical forms'; paragraph 5 draws the lesson that we should judge a portrait 'by the question it was asked to answer'.

Why not (A): The author ranks neither period; paragraph 5 says modern portraits also display position and older ones could show character.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 says photography took over 'much of the trade in cheap likenesses', one of several changes; it does not say the market for painted portraits was destroyed, and this is not the passage's focus.

Why not (C): The symbols in paragraph 2 support the claim that portraits established position; listing them is too narrow to be the passage's concern.

Common misconception: Choosing a supporting detail (photography, symbols) or turning the author's relativising point into a ranking.

Difficulty: The thesis is explicit in paragraph 4 but the wrong options draw on real material from paragraphs 2, 4 and 5. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-024 · Visual art and museums · passage Difficult · 585 words · 5 questions

Anyone who watches visitors in a busy gallery will notice how briefly most pictures are looked at. A few seconds for the image, a little longer for the label, and the visitor moves on. In response, many museums now promote what is sometimes called slow looking: sessions in which participants are invited to sit before a single work for a quarter of an hour or more, noting whatever comes to their attention. The premise is simple and attractive. We miss what is in paintings because we do not give them time, and if we gave them time, they would show us more.

The practice has real merits, and its critics should concede them. It interrupts the habit, encouraged by crowded rooms and handheld screens, of treating a picture as something to be recognised and ticked off. It gives permission to stay, which many visitors seem to need. And it does sometimes yield discoveries: a figure in the background that had gone unnoticed, a repeated colour that ties distant parts of the canvas together. But the premise behind it, that duration by itself turns into perception, is weaker than it looks. Fifteen minutes in front of a canvas can be fifteen minutes of reverie, of thinking about lunch, or of staring with growing unease at a surface that refuses to become interesting. Time is a condition of seeing, not a cause of it.

What turns time into seeing is a question. The art historian who notices in a moment that the light in a landscape falls from two directions does not see faster because her eyes are better; she sees because she has learned to ask where the light comes from, and the painting answers. Expertise, in this sense, is a stock of questions that direct the eye, questions that are not mysterious endowments but are acquired, often laboriously, through the comparison of many works, and that can therefore in principle be taught. This explains why experts often see in seconds what a patient beginner misses in an hour. The beginner is not looking less; he is looking without a question, and an unguided gaze tends to settle on whatever is largest, brightest or most familiar.

Yet the expert's advantage carries a risk of its own, and here the advocates of slow looking have a point that their own premise obscures. A stock of questions is also a stock of expectations. The historian who knows what to look for in a painting of a certain school may stop looking once she has found it, filing the picture under its category and failing to notice what does not fit. Questions direct the eye, but they also tell it where it need not go. Some of the most valuable observations a viewer can make come from setting expertise aside long enough to be surprised, and slow looking at its best is precisely a technique for producing that suspension.

The two halves of the matter therefore need each other. Time without questions drifts; questions without time confirm what they expected. Neither ingredient is dispensable, and neither, taken in isolation, is sufficient for the kind of perception that the advocates of slow looking rightly prize. The most useful practice is neither the patient blankness that slow looking sometimes encourages nor the quick classification that expertise permits, but an alternation between them: a period of asking, a period of letting the asking lapse, and a return to the picture to see whether it has said something the questions did not predict.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says it 'does sometimes yield discoveries' and that fifteen minutes can be spent in reverie; there is no guarantee.

Common misconception: Upgrading 'sometimes' to a guarantee, or crediting slow looking with what paragraph 3 attributes to expertise.

CAT-VARC-P07-025 · Music and performance · passage Easy · 456 words · 4 questions

The word improvisation tends to conjure an image of pure spontaneity: a musician who sits down with nothing prepared and lets whatever arrives come out. Listeners who admire improvisers often praise them in exactly these terms, as if the value of the performance lay in its having come from nowhere. Yet the image badly misdescribes what improvising musicians actually do. In the classical traditions of the Indian subcontinent, a performer exploring a raga is bound by an intricate set of expectations: which notes may be used, how the scale is approached in ascent and in descent, which phrases characterise the raga and which would make it sound like a neighbouring one. A jazz soloist working through a familiar tune is likewise held by the sequence of chords beneath the melody, by the length of each chorus and by a large shared stock of phrases that players absorb over years of listening.

What these traditions share is not an absence of rules but a particular relation to them. The improviser has internalised the constraints so thoroughly that they no longer need to be consulted; they shape choices in the way grammar shapes the sentences of a fluent speaker, who does not pause to recall the rules before talking. The comparison with language is useful because it removes an apparent paradox. Nobody thinks that a conversation is unoriginal because it obeys grammar, and nobody thinks that grammar prevents people from saying things that have never been said before. On the contrary, it is the shared structure that makes a new sentence intelligible as new.

This has a practical consequence for how improvisation is learned. A student who is told simply to be free usually produces something shapeless, or falls back on a handful of habits picked up by accident. Teachers in both traditions instead begin with imitation: the student copies phrases, sometimes for years, until the vocabulary becomes second nature. To an outsider this can look like the opposite of creativity. But imitation here is the means by which the constraints are absorbed, and only once they have been absorbed can the student vary them with confidence.

None of this is to say that improvisation is merely the recombination of stored parts, or that the best improvisers are simply those with the largest memories. What listeners value in a great performance is often a moment at which a convention is stretched or bent in a way that still makes sense within the tradition, so that the surprise is felt as a surprise. Such moments are possible only for someone who knows exactly where the boundaries lie. Freedom in improvisation, in other words, is not freedom from form but a fluency so complete that form can be played with.

CAT-VARC-P07-025-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · MediumMCQ
The primary purpose of the passage is to
  1. (A)challenge a popular picture of improvisation by arguing that its freedom rests on conventions the performer has fully absorbed.
  2. (B)argue that improvisers are best understood as skilled recombiners of phrases that they have memorised during long training.
  3. (C)show that improvisation in Indian classical music is more tightly governed by rules than improvisation in jazz.
  4. (D)recommend that teachers of improvisation stop encouraging students to imitate and instead train them to recall the rules.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 names the image of 'pure spontaneity' and says it 'badly misdescribes' improvisers; paragraphs 2 and 3 explain that the constraints are internalised like grammar; paragraph 4 concludes that freedom is 'a fluency so complete that form can be played with'. The purpose is to correct the popular picture.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 explicitly denies this: 'None of this is to say that improvisation is merely the recombination of stored parts'.

Why not (C): The passage presents the raga and the jazz tune as parallel cases of the same point ('What these traditions share'); it never ranks one tradition as more rule-bound.

Why not (D): The passage endorses imitation as 'the means by which the constraints are absorbed' and says fluent players do not consult rules, so the recommendation reverses its view.

Common misconception: Taking the comparison of two traditions, or the stress on imitation, for the purpose, or overstating the thesis into the claim that improvising is only recombination.

Difficulty: One wrong option (recombination) uses the passage's own emphasis on absorbed vocabulary and must be rejected through the concession in paragraph 4. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-025-Q4 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following is most closely analogous to the author's account of what makes a great improvised moment?
  1. (A)A poet who has written strict sonnets for decades breaks the expected rhyme in the final line, and readers feel the break as deliberate and apt.
  2. (B)A beginner who has never studied verse writes a poem with no metre or rhyme, and some readers find its roughness refreshing and honest.
  3. (C)A poet with an exceptional memory for other people's verse assembles a new poem entirely from lines borrowed from famous earlier poems.
  4. (D)A translator who looks up every word in a dictionary produces a version that is accurate in each line but stiff when read aloud as a whole.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 4 says listeners value 'a moment at which a convention is stretched or bent in a way that still makes sense within the tradition', possible 'only for someone who knows exactly where the boundaries lie'. The sonneteer has internalised the form and departs from it at one point in a way readers recognise as meaningful.

Why not (B): The author says a performer without absorbed constraints 'usually produces something shapeless'; this case lacks the mastery of form that the passage makes essential.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 denies that the best improvisers are 'simply those with the largest memories' or that improvising is 'the recombination of stored parts'.

Why not (D): This depicts rules consulted rather than internalised, which the passage contrasts with fluency; it involves no meaningful bending of a convention.

Common misconception: Choosing the untrained poet because the passage praises freedom, or the memorising poet because it stresses absorbed vocabulary.

Difficulty: Each wrong option matches one strand of the passage (freedom, rules, memory); only the correct answer matches the full account. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-026 · Music and performance · passage Easy · 424 words · 4 questions

Of all the performing arts, dance has the most fragile relation to its own past. A play survives in its script and a symphony in its score, so that later generations, whatever they make of these documents, at least have something to argue over. A dance, by contrast, has for most of its history existed only while it was being danced and in the memories of those who danced it. It is tempting to conclude that dance is therefore an art without a history, or one whose history is a long series of losses, and that the remedy is simply better documentation.

There have been serious attempts at such documentation. Systems of dance notation have been devised that record the position of each part of the body over time, and film and video now capture performances that would once have vanished. These tools are valuable, but they have not displaced the older method of transmission, in which a dancer who has performed a role teaches it directly to a younger dancer, in a studio, by demonstration and correction. Many companies that hold detailed records of their repertoire still rely on such teaching when a work is revived, and they tend to treat the written record as an aid to memory rather than as the work itself.

The reason is not mere conservatism. What passes from one body to another in the studio includes things that a notation or a recording captures poorly or not at all: the weight of a movement, the quality of attention behind it, the reasons a gesture is timed as it is. A film shows what a particular dancer did on a particular night, including her idiosyncrasies and her mistakes; it cannot say which of those features belong to the choreography and which belong to her. A teacher who has danced the role can make that distinction, or at least has a considered view about it, and passes the view on along with the steps.

This method has an obvious cost. Each transmission is also an interpretation, and over several generations a dance may drift a long way from what its maker first set. Yet the drift is not simply a corruption. A dance kept alive in bodies is continually tested against the dancers who perform it, and the features that survive are usually those that dancers have found to matter. The archive of dance, on this view, is not mainly a shelf of documents but a lineage of people, and its gaps are the price of its being alive.

  3. (C)still revive works through direct teaching and use the written record to support memory.
  4. (D)have abandoned studio teaching now that film and video can capture complete performances.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 2: such companies 'still rely on such teaching when a work is revived, and they tend to treat the written record as an aid to memory rather than as the work itself'.

Why not (A): No such use of notation is described; paragraph 4 discusses drift without proposing documents as a corrective.

Why not (B): The passage says companies treat the record 'as an aid to memory rather than as the work itself', the reverse of making it authoritative.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says these tools 'have not displaced the older method of transmission'; companies 'still rely on such teaching'.

Common misconception: Assuming that better tools must have replaced older methods.

Difficulty: Located in one sentence of paragraph 2; wrong options contradict it plainly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-027 · Music and performance · passage Medium · 405 words · 4 questions

It is often said that the phonograph and its successors simply made music available, carrying performances that had been confined to concert halls into the homes of people who would never have heard them. The claim is true as far as it goes, and the gain it describes is enormous: a listener in a small town can now hear more music in a month than a devoted concertgoer of earlier centuries could hope to hear in years. But the image of recording as a window, through which the listener observes a performance that would have happened in much the same way anyway, conceals the way recording altered both the performance and the act of listening.

Consider first what repetition does. A live performance is heard once; its slips pass and are forgotten, and its moments of risk are experienced as risk. A recording is heard again and again, and on the tenth hearing a wrong note or a wavering tone is no longer a passing accident but a permanent blemish. Performers who recorded learned that what was acceptable, even exciting, in a hall could become intolerable on disc. As editing techniques improved, it became possible to assemble a single flawless performance from many takes, and the flawless performance became the standard against which the live one was measured.

Listeners, in turn, came to the concert hall carrying recordings in their heads. Many now know a work first, and sometimes only, through one recorded interpretation, and they hear a live performance as a departure from it. This is a curious inversion. The recording was meant to be a copy of the performance; it has become, for many listeners, the original, and the performance its imperfect copy. Some performers have responded by playing more cautiously, aiming at the clean surface that audiences have learned to expect, at the cost of the spontaneity that once distinguished the live event.

It would be wrong to treat this as a simple story of decline. Recording has also allowed listeners to compare interpretations in a way no earlier audience could, and it has preserved musical traditions that might otherwise have vanished with their last practitioners. The point is rather that recording is not neutral. A technology that lets us hear the same performance indefinitely changes what we think a performance is for, and an account of modern musical life that leaves this out has mistaken a transformation for a mere extension.

Why not (D): The passage does not rank recording artists above concert performers; the same performers learned that slips acceptable in a hall became 'intolerable on disc'.

Common misconception: Supplying an outside explanation (attention, equipment) instead of the one the passage gives.

Difficulty: The cause is stated in adjacent sentences of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-028 · Music and performance · passage Medium · 419 words · 4 questions

Few facts about music are more familiar, or more puzzling on reflection, than the willingness of listeners to seek out music that makes them sad. People do not, on the whole, go looking for grief or disappointment in the rest of life, and they would think it odd to be offered a sorrowful afternoon as a treat. Yet slow laments, songs of loss and the darker movements of symphonies are among the most loved music in many traditions, and listeners return to them deliberately.

One response dissolves the puzzle by denying its premise. On this view the listener does not really feel sad at all; she recognises sadness in the music, much as she might recognise a sorrowful expression on a carved mask, and what she enjoys is the beauty and skill with which the sadness is expressed. The view has the merit of explaining why sad music does not ruin anyone's day. But it fits poorly with what many listeners report, which is that they are moved, sometimes to tears, and not merely informed. A second response accepts that the feeling is real and explains the pleasure by the frame around it: the sadness arrives without a loss to cause it and without consequences to follow, so it can be savoured in a way that the sadness of life cannot.

Both responses capture something, but both treat the emotion as a single state, present or absent, mild or intense. What music does with an emotion is closer to giving it a shape over time. A lament does not simply announce sorrow; it gathers, rises, breaks, returns to a phrase heard earlier and comes to rest. The listener who follows it is led through a version of sorrow that has a beginning, an end and a recognisable order, which is precisely what the sorrows of ordinary life usually lack. The pleasure, on this account, lies less in the feeling than in the form the feeling is given.

This does not mean that sad music always pleases, or pleases everyone. Many people who are grieving find that they cannot listen to the music they loved before, at least for a time, and their experience is a useful check on any theory. A frame that keeps sadness at a safe distance should make such music easier to bear in grief, not harder. The account offered here does better: a shaped and completed sorrow may be unbearable to someone whose own sorrow has no shape yet, because the contrast is too sharp.

Common misconception: Attributing a reported view to the author, or attaching the example to the wrong view.

Difficulty: The example sits inside a clearly labelled account of the first view. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-029 · Music and performance · passage Medium · 400 words · 4 questions

During the twentieth century a movement grew up among performers of European music written before about 1800 that sought to recover how that music had sounded when it was new. Its adherents played on instruments built to older designs, or on surviving originals: harpsichords rather than modern pianos, gut strings rather than steel, wooden flutes without the elaborate keywork of later models. They studied treatises on performance written in the period and drew from them conclusions about tempo, ornamentation and the size of ensembles. Much of the result was a revelation. Textures that had sounded thick and heavy when played by large modern orchestras became transparent, and pieces long heard as dutiful exercises began to sound witty and alive.

The difficulty lies in the word that the movement often attached to itself, authenticity. If authenticity means sounding as the music sounded to its first audiences, the goal is in one important respect unreachable. Even if every instrument and every tempo could be reconstructed exactly, the listeners could not be. An audience that has heard the music of the following two centuries hears an older piece against all of it; a dissonance that once startled may now pass unnoticed, and a small ensemble that once seemed ordinary may now sound austere and pointed. The same sounds, entering differently formed ears, do not produce the same experience.

A second difficulty concerns the composers' own wishes. It is sometimes assumed that a composer who wrote for the instruments of his day would have wanted exactly those instruments, and that a performance on them is therefore more faithful to his intentions. But composers wrote for what was available, and some of them are known to have been dissatisfied with the instruments and players they had. To infer a preference from a circumstance is to confuse what a composer was given with what he would have chosen.

None of this tells against the performances themselves, many of which are among the most vivid of the past century. It tells against a particular way of justifying them. The best of these performances persuade not because they are correct, in the sense of matching a lost original, but because the research behind them led performers to hear possibilities that habit had concealed. Historical evidence used in this way is less a rulebook than a provocation: a reason to try something, which must then succeed or fail as music.

  4. (D)dissonances that later audiences had stopped noticing began to startle listeners once again.

Common misconception: Picking a detail about dissonance from paragraph 2 and reversing it.

CAT-VARC-P07-029-Q3 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
Which of the following arguments makes the same error that paragraph 3 identifies?
  1. (A)A playwright's audiences knew stories that modern audiences do not, so modern productions cannot recreate the effect the plays first had.
  2. (B)A playwright's early productions were praised by critics, so modern productions that copy them will be equally admired today.
  3. (C)A playwright whose plays were first staged in small rooms lit by candles must have wanted them staged that way, so modern productions should do the same.
  4. (D)A playwright left detailed notes on how certain scenes should be staged, so directors who follow those notes are closer to the writer's stated wishes.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 criticises the step from the fact that a composer wrote for the instruments available to the conclusion that he wanted exactly those instruments: 'To infer a preference from a circumstance'. The candle-lit staging argument makes the same step.

Why not (A): This parallels paragraph 2's point about differently formed listeners, which the author endorses; it is not the error of inferring a preference from a circumstance.

Why not (B): This assumes that past success guarantees present success, a different flaw from confusing 'what a composer was given with what he would have chosen'.

Why not (D): Here the preference is documented, not inferred from what happened to be available, so the inference does not commit the error described.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about audiences, which matches a different argument in the passage.

Difficulty: One wrong option mirrors the passage's other objection, so the solver must identify which reasoning paragraph 3 attacks. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-030 · Music and performance · passage Medium · 424 words · 4 questions

When collectors began, in the nineteenth century, to travel through the countryside writing down the songs of rural singers, they often approached their task as an act of rescue. The songs, they believed, were ancient and dying, and each singer they encountered was a damaged witness to an original that had once existed in a purer and more complete form. Variation between versions was evidence of decay: a verse forgotten here, a line garbled there, a tune that had wandered from its proper shape. The collector's responsibility, accordingly, was to compare the surviving fragments and reconstruct, as far as the evidence permitted, the song as it had existed before the damage began.

This picture borrowed its method from the editing of ancient manuscripts, where it makes good sense. A classical text was written once by a particular author, and later copies really are more or less faithful to it. A folk song is a fundamentally different kind of object. It was never fixed in a single authoritative form; it existed only in individual performances, each of which was shaped by the singer's memory, temperament and taste, and by the particular audience and occasion. A singer who shortened a ballad for a restless crowd, or changed the name of a town to one her listeners knew, was not corrupting a text. She was doing what singers of such songs had always done, and what had made the songs worth keeping.

Seen this way, variation is not the symptom of a song's decline but the very mechanism of its survival. Songs that could be adapted to unfamiliar places and new purposes travelled widely; songs that resisted such adaptation were gradually forgotten. The very versions the collectors regarded as degraded were often the ones in which a song was most alive, because they showed it being fitted to the people who still sang it. There is an irony here. By printing a single standard text, often assembled from several singers, the collectors gave the songs the fixed form they had never had, and later performers sometimes learned from the printed page instead of from other singers.

It would nevertheless be unfair to dismiss the collectors' work on this account. Without their notebooks a great many songs would be irrecoverably lost, and some of them took care to record variants and the names of the singers they heard. The lesson is narrower. A record of oral music is valuable precisely to the extent that it preserves the variety which the collectors' theory taught them to regard as noise.

CAT-VARC-P07-030-Q1 · Inference · EasyMCQ
The passage suggests that a singer who replaced the name of a town in a ballad with one her listeners knew was

  2. (B)damaging the song in the way the collectors feared, though for an understandable reason.
  3. (C)departing from the custom of her tradition, which required songs to be sung as they were learned.
  4. (D)making an error that later singers corrected when they learned the song from printed texts.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 2: the singer who 'changed the name of a town to one her listeners knew, was not corrupting a text. She was doing what singers of such songs had always done'.

Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says such a singer 'was not corrupting a text'; the author rejects the collectors' view of change as damage.

Why not (C): The passage says she 'was doing what singers of such songs had always done', so she was following the custom, not breaking it.

Why not (D): The passage mentions later singers learning from print as an irony of fixing the songs, not as a correction of earlier errors.

Common misconception: Adopting the collectors' view of variation, which the passage reports in order to reject.

Difficulty: The relevant sentences state the point almost directly. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-030-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ

CAT-VARC-P07-031 · Music and performance · passage Difficult · 617 words · 5 questions

A familiar way of thinking about Western art music divides the creative labour with reassuring neatness. The composer makes the work and records it in a score; the performer's task is to realise the score faithfully, adding as little of himself as the music allows. On this view the score is the work, or at least its complete specification, and a performance is good to the degree that it delivers what the notation contains. Its attractions are considerable. It accounts for our confidence that two strikingly different performances are nevertheless performances of the same composition, and it furnishes the critic with a standard: the performer who alters the notes has made a mistake, whatever the result sounds like.

The difficulty is that notation specifies far less than the view requires. A score fixes pitches and their order with considerable precision, and durations rather less, but a great deal of what listeners hear as the character of a performance is left open or indicated only roughly. A marking that asks for a loud passage does not say how loud, and the right loudness in a small room is not the right loudness in a large hall. A word indicating a lively tempo admits a range of speeds wide enough to make one performance sound urgent and another relaxed. Nothing in the marks tells the player exactly how long to linger at the high point of a phrase, or how to weigh the notes of a chord against one another. These are not trivial details that a faithful performer can safely settle at random; they are much of what makes the music sound the way it does.

One response is to swing to the opposite extreme and say that the work simply is the performance, the score being no more than a set of instructions that each performer completes as she pleases. This rescues the performer's creative contribution, but at a considerable theoretical price. It cannot explain why some performances strike informed listeners as wrong-headed even when every note is in place, or why a performer who takes a slow movement at double speed has done something worse than make an unusual choice. If the score were merely a recipe to be finished at will, there would be no ground for such judgements, and yet musicians make them constantly and with considerable agreement.

That agreement points to what both positions conspicuously omit. Scores are never interpreted in isolation. They are read by musicians trained in a practice: a shared and largely unwritten understanding of what the marks mean in a given style, which conventions apply, what kinds of freedom are expected and where those freedoms stop. In music of one period a trill is understood to begin on the note above; in another it begins on the main note; the score is usually silent on the point, and the practice supplies the answer. The work, on this account, is neither the marks alone nor whatever a given performance makes of them, but the marks as a practice reads them. Fidelity is owed to that combination.

It may be objected that practices evolve, so that the work would apparently evolve with them, which seems absurd. But the objection establishes considerably less than it appears to. Practices do change, slowly, and the identity of a work is correspondingly less fixed than the first view supposed; that is a cost worth accepting, since it matches how musicians actually argue about performances. What does not follow is that anything goes. A practice at any moment draws lines that its members can defend, and a performer who knowingly crosses them is making a claim that must be argued for, not exercising a right.

  2. (B)The pitches of the notes and the order in which they come.
  3. (C)How loud a passage should be in a given performance space.
  4. (D)How long a performer should linger at the high point of a phrase.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says a marking for a loud passage 'does not say how loud', and the right loudness depends on the room.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says 'Nothing in the marks tells the player exactly how long to linger at the high point of a phrase'.

Common misconception: Choosing a feature that a score does mark (loudness, tempo) without noticing that the passage says it is marked only roughly.

CAT-VARC-P07-032 · Music and performance · passage Difficult · 595 words · 5 questions

Defenders of live theatre, when pressed to explain why anyone should purchase an expensive ticket to see what could be watched more cheaply and comfortably on a screen, usually appeal to presence. The actors are there, in the same room, breathing the same air; on screen they are only images. The answer is sincere, but considered as an argument it is considerably weaker than it sounds. A great many things are physically present to us without being valuable for that reason, and a lecture delivered in person is not obviously better than the same lecture recorded. If presence were all that distinguished the stage, the case for theatre would rest on something close to a superstition about physical proximity.

A more promising answer begins from what presence makes possible. A filmed performance is complete before any viewer encounters it; nothing the viewer does, however attentive or distracted, can reach back and alter it. A stage performance is still being made while the audience watches, and the audience is among the conditions under which it is made. Actors in a comedy learn to hold a line until the laughter at the previous one has crested and begun to fall, and the length of that pause differs from night to night because the laughter does. In a tense scene, a silence of a particular quality in the house tells the performers that they have the room, and they may let a moment stretch further than they would before a restless audience. These adjustments are individually small, but cumulatively they mean that a stage performance can be, in part, a response to the particular people assembled to witness it.

The audience, moreover, is conscious of this reciprocity. Spectators in a theatre are aware that they can be heard and, in an intimate space, seen; they are also aware of one another, and a laugh that spreads along a row, or fails to, shapes what each person feels. The experience is less that of watching an object than of taking part, in a minor and mostly silent way, in an event whose outcome is not wholly settled. That is why a performance can fail in the theatre in a way that a film cannot: not through a flaw in its making, which a film may also have, but through a breakdown between stage and house.

It may be objected that this account flatters most actual theatre, which is rarely so delicately attuned to its spectators. Many productions are drilled to such precision that they vary little from night to night, and in a large commercial house the performers can barely see past the first rows. That is true, and it calls for a qualification rather than a retraction. The responsiveness described here is a capacity of the live form, not a guarantee; a production can choose to use it or to suppress it, and one that suppresses it entirely has given up the main thing that the stage offers and the screen does not. Film, meanwhile, has resources of its own, the close-up and the edit among them, and nothing here implies that it is a lesser art.

The case for live theatre, then, is not that the actors are physically near but that the performance and its audience remain capable of affecting one another. It follows, perhaps uncomfortably for some institutions, that the strongest argument for theatre is simultaneously a demand upon it. A theatre that wishes to justify its prices by appealing to liveness owes its audience a performance in which liveness actually does some work.
