Why not (B): Paragraph 2 says 'A more promising answer begins from what presence makes possible', so the author builds the better argument from the weak one.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says 'Film ... has resources of its own, the close-up and the edit among them'.

Common misconception: Choosing the presence option because the author criticises the appeal to presence, missing that paragraph 2 develops it into the better answer.

Difficulty: The correct answer sounds sympathetic to the author's account of liveness; it is refuted only by the qualification in paragraph 4, while one wrong option seems to clash with paragraph 1 but is endorsed in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-033 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Easy · 424 words · 4 questions

When a person pushes a door that should have been pulled, the usual verdict is that the person was careless, and the verdict is so automatic that we rarely notice how strange it is. A door has one job that involves its user, which is to tell an approaching hand what to do, and a door that sends the wrong message has failed at that job as surely as a door that sticks in its frame. Yet the failure is booked to the person, who apologises and blushes, while the door goes on misleading everyone who comes after.

The habit of blaming users has a simple source, which is that designers judge their objects from the inside. They know which way the door swings because they drew it, and they know which button on the oven sets the timer because they chose its position. What is obvious to someone who already knows the answer tells us very little about what an object communicates to someone meeting it for the first time, in a hurry, with a bag in one hand. A flat plate invites pushing and a vertical bar invites pulling, whatever the small sign above them says. When the hardware and the sign disagree, people follow the hardware, and they are not foolish to do so, since the shape of a thing is read faster than words are.

There is a practical cost to the blaming habit beyond the irritation it causes. Errors that are attributed to individuals are treated by asking individuals to try harder: more signs, more training, more warnings printed in red. Errors that are attributed to design are treated by changing the design, which removes the error for everyone at once. Imagine a hospital in which two different medicines are supplied in bottles of the same size and colour. If it blames the nurse who confuses them, it may discipline that nurse and still find the confusion repeated next year by someone else; if it makes the bottles look different, it removes the occasion for the mistake altogether.

This is not to say that every mistake is the designer's fault, or that objects can be made so clear that nobody ever misuses them. Some tasks are intrinsically demanding, and some carelessness is simply carelessness. But the sensible default runs the other way from the usual one. When many people make the same mistake with the same object, the repetition is itself a kind of evidence, and it points less at the people than at the thing they all share.

CAT-VARC-P07-033-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ

  2. (B)every error that people make with everyday objects is the fault of the designers who made those objects.
  3. (C)written signs and printed warnings are useless and should be removed from doors, appliances and public buildings.
  4. (D)designers ought to test every new product on hurried first-time users before it is allowed to reach the market.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 questions the automatic verdict of carelessness, paragraph 2 explains why designers misjudge what their objects say, paragraph 3 shows the cost of blaming users, and paragraph 4 concludes that repeated mistakes point 'less at the people than at the thing they all share'. The correct answer states this central claim with its limit.

Why not (B): Paragraph 4 explicitly denies that 'every mistake is the designer's fault' and allows that 'some carelessness is simply carelessness'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says only that people follow the hardware when it disagrees with the sign; it does not call signs useless or propose removing them, so the option is extreme.

Why not (D): The passage explains why designers misjudge their objects but never proposes a testing rule; the option turns a diagnosis into a prescription the author does not make.

Common misconception: Picking the absolute version of the thesis and missing the concession in paragraph 4.

CAT-VARC-P07-034 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Easy · 402 words · 4 questions

Admirers of vernacular building, the houses and barns put up by people who were not architects, often describe them as stores of climatic wisdom. The thick earth walls of a desert village keep rooms cool through the afternoon and release their stored warmth at night; the steep roofs of a snowy valley shed their load before it can crush the rafters; the raised floors of a flood plain let the water pass beneath. Each form, on this account, is the answer a community reached after centuries of trial, and modern builders, with their sealed glass boxes and mechanical cooling, would do well to copy it.

There is much truth in this, but the story is told too kindly. The forms that survive to be admired are the ones that worked well enough to be kept; the failures, the houses that collapsed and the villages abandoned after a bad decade, left nothing for us to photograph. What we see is therefore a filtered record, and it flatters the past. More important, vernacular forms were shaped by poverty and scarcity as much as by climate. People built with earth because earth lay underfoot and cost nothing to carry, not because they had compared it with every other material and found it best. A thick wall may be cool, but it may also be dark, damp in the rains and laborious to patch every year, and those who lived behind it bore those costs whether or not their admirers mention them.

None of this makes vernacular building less worth studying. It suggests, however, that the lesson lies in the method rather than in the forms. What village builders did well was to adjust slowly and continuously to the conditions in front of them, keeping what worked and changing what did not, with materials whose behaviour they knew intimately because they handled them every season. Their knowledge was local by necessity, and its strength was precisely that it never pretended to be anything else.

A modern builder who copies the outward shape of a courtyard house in a different climate, with different materials and a different way of living, has learned nothing from it, however picturesque the result. One who studies local sun, wind and rain with the same patience, builds, watches what happens and is willing to revise, has learned a great deal, even if the building that results looks nothing like a village house.

  4. (D)wholly approving, since the author endorses their call for modern builders to copy traditional forms directly in their own work.
Answer and explanation

Paragraph 2 opens 'There is much truth in this, but the story is told too kindly', then explains how the record is filtered and how scarcity shaped the forms. The author keeps the account's insight while rejecting its idealisation.

Why not (A): The author grants 'There is much truth in this' and does not dispute that thick walls keep rooms cool; the objection is to how kindly the story is told.

Why not (B): The author says vernacular building remains 'worth studying' and draws a lesson from it for modern builders, so indifference is wrong.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says a builder who copies the outward shape of a courtyard house elsewhere 'has learned nothing from it', which rejects that call.

Common misconception: Reading the criticism in paragraph 2 as a total rejection and overlooking 'much truth'.

Difficulty: The tone is announced in a single signposted sentence at the start of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-035 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Medium · 430 words · 4 questions

For much of the last century it was an article of faith among progressive architects that ornament was a kind of lie. A building, they argued, should declare its structure and its purpose plainly; carving and moulding added cost without adding use, and they disguised what a building really was beneath a costume borrowed from some other age. The argument had moral force, and it produced buildings of great clarity. It also rested on a narrow idea of what counts as use.

Consider what ornament has traditionally done: a carved surround tells a stranger which of many openings is the entrance, so that nobody needs to read a sign to find the way in. A band of moulding at the level of the first floor breaks a tall wall into parts that a human eye can measure, so that a passer-by senses the height of the building in storeys rather than as an undifferentiated cliff. Projecting mouldings over windows throw rain clear of the glass and, as they weather, gather their stains in a pattern that reads as intended rather than as decay. Even purely figurative ornament, a row of leaves or a face above a door, offers the eye something to rest on at walking pace, which is the pace at which most people meet most buildings. These are uses, though not of the kind that appear in a structural calculation.

The critics were right about one thing: ornament applied without regard to any of these purposes, stuck on to a facade like a label on a jar, is as dishonest as they said, and much commercial building of their day was exactly that. But the remedy they chose, to strip surfaces bare, threw away the work along with the pretence. A blank wall is not neutral, for it tells the stranger nothing about where to go in, it offers no measure of scale, and it weathers into streaks that look like neglect because nothing in its design anticipated them. The plain building is honest about its structure and silent about almost everything else that a person on the street needs to know.

The more useful question, then, is not whether a building should be ornamented but what its ornament is for. Asked in that way, the question rules out a good deal of pastiche, the reproduction of historical detail for its associations alone. It also rules out a good deal of austerity that passes itself off as rigour, and it returns the judgement of a facade to the people who have to find their way past it.

CAT-VARC-P07-036 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Medium · 410 words · 4 questions

A curious reversal has taken place in the market for handmade goods. For most of history, the aim of a skilled potter or weaver was regularity: bowls that stacked, threads of even thickness, a pattern that repeated without drift. Irregularity was the mark of an apprentice or of a hurried job, and the master's pride lay in eliminating it. Today, by contrast, the small wobble in a glaze or the slight lean of a hand-thrown cup is what many buyers are paying for. It has become the proof that a person, and not a machine, was involved.

The trouble with this proof is that it can be forged. A machine that can make a perfect cup can be programmed to make an imperfect one, and to vary the imperfection from piece to piece so that no two look alike. Tableware with deliberately uneven rims and glazes is already produced in quantity, and an ordinary buyer cannot tell it from the real thing. If what we value in the handmade is irregularity as such, then the handmade has no defence against its imitation, and the premium paid for it is a premium paid for a story.

But irregularity was never the point, and treating it as the point misunderstands what a craftsperson does. A turner working a piece of wood meets a knot, a change in the grain, a softer patch, and alters the cut to suit it; a weaver feels the tension of the warp change with the damp in the air and corrects for it. The handmade object is the record of many such small judgements, each a response to the particular material in front of the maker. Its variations, where they occur, are traces of that responsiveness, not ornaments added afterwards. A machine-made irregularity, however convincing, responds to nothing; it is a pattern generated in advance and imposed on material treated as uniform.

This distinction can be pressed too far. Machines can be built to sense and respond to their material, and a good deal of hand production is as mechanical as anything in a factory, the same motion repeated on identical parts without a thought. The line between craft and its imitation, then, is not drawn by the presence of a hand. It is drawn by whether the making involved attention to the particular, and by that test some machine work would count as craft while much of what is sold as handmade would not.

CAT-VARC-P07-036-Q4 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
By the test proposed in the final paragraph, which of the following would most clearly count as craft?
  1. (A)A factory press that stamps a different random dent into every metal bowl so that no two bowls are ever identical.
  2. (B)A bread machine that measures the moisture of each batch of flour and changes its kneading time to suit that batch.

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 4 says the line 'is not drawn by the presence of a hand' but by 'whether the making involved attention to the particular', and that machines 'can be built to sense and respond to their material'. The bread machine responds to each batch, so by that test it counts as craft.

Why not (A): This is the forged irregularity of paragraph 2: a pattern 'generated in advance and imposed on material treated as uniform', responding to nothing.

Why not (C): The knitting is done by hand but without 'attention to the particular' material, which paragraph 4 says decides the matter, not the hand.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 names this as hand production 'as mechanical as anything in a factory', which the test excludes.

Common misconception: Choosing a handmade option because it involves a hand, which the final paragraph says is not the criterion.

Difficulty: Counter-intuitive answer: the only machine option is right and the hand-made options are wrong, which requires applying the final test strictly. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-037 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Medium · 412 words · 4 questions

Every few generations someone tries to rescue a dying craft by writing it down. A retiring stonemason or bell founder is interviewed at length, his methods are photographed and measured, and the results are published as a manual that, it is hoped, will allow the craft to survive its last practitioners. The manuals are often admirable documents, careful, accurate and lovingly illustrated. They are also, as anyone who has tried to learn from one discovers, strangely inert: the reader can follow every step and still produce work that a practitioner would reject at a glance.

The reason is that much of what a skilled worker knows is not a set of steps at all but a way of perceiving. A smith judges the heat of iron by its colour, and the relevant differences of colour are ones that a novice literally does not see; a joiner hears in the note of a mallet whether a joint has seated; a plasterer feels through the trowel the moment at which the mix has begun to stiffen. Such judgements can be described, but the description presupposes the perception it describes. An instruction to work the metal when it turns the colour of straw is useful only to someone who already knows which straw, in which light, and how much variation is allowed.

Apprenticeship solved this problem not by explaining more but by arranging for the learner to perceive under guidance. The apprentice stood beside the master for years and was told 'now' and 'not yet' thousands of times, until he slowly came to see what the master saw. The words mattered, but chiefly as pointers to experiences the learner was having at that very moment. What passed from one generation to the next was less a body of information than a trained attention.

It would be sentimental to conclude that apprenticeship should simply be restored. It was slow, it was often exploitative, and the guilds that ran it guarded their knowledge as much to keep out competitors as to preserve standards. Nor are manuals useless: to a learner who has already begun to see, a good written account can organise and extend what practice has started. The lesson is narrower. A craft cannot be stored in a text in the way a recipe can, because what has to be preserved is a capacity of persons, and the only reliable way to keep it alive is to keep people practising it alongside others who already can.

CAT-VARC-P07-038 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Medium · 404 words · 4 questions

When an old building is to be repaired, those responsible must first decide what, exactly, they are repairing it to. One answer, long popular and still widely held, is that the building should be returned to its original state, or to the state its first builders intended. Later additions are stripped away, missing parts are reconstructed from evidence or from informed guesses, and the result is a building more complete and more consistent than it has been for centuries. The appeal is obvious: the visitor sees the church or the fort as it was meant to be seen, whole and coherent.

The opposing answer holds that a building's history does not stop at its completion. A medieval hall that later acquired a Georgian window, a Victorian staircase and a twentieth-century kitchen is a document of all those periods, and to strip it back to its first state is to erase most of what it records. On this view the repairer's duty is to keep what has survived, to intervene as little as possible, and to make any new work distinguishable from the old, so that no one mistakes a modern guess for an original fact. The restored building, by contrast, presents the restorer's idea of the past as though it were the past itself.

I find the second answer more persuasive, but it is not as neutral as its defenders sometimes suggest. Not every layer is worth keeping: a cheap partition put up in a hurry may hide a painted ceiling of real importance, and refusing to remove it is itself a choice about which history matters. Keeping everything is also an interpretation, one that values continuity over quality and treats the accident of survival as a kind of verdict. The conservator, no less than the restorer, decides what the building will say.

What separates the two approaches, then, is not that one interprets and the other does not. It is that conservation, at its best, leaves its interpretations visible and reversible, so that a later generation with better evidence or different priorities can undo them. Restoration tends to make its choices permanent and to conceal them, since a convincing reconstruction is one in which the seams cannot be found. Because every generation has been confident about the past and most have been wrong about some of it, the approach that allows its mistakes to be corrected deserves the benefit of the doubt.

Why not (B): Cost is never mentioned; paragraph 2 gives the reason as preventing anyone from mistaking 'a modern guess for an original fact'.

Common misconception: Attributing the restorers' goal from paragraph 1 to the conservers.

Difficulty: The reason is given in a single clause of paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-039 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Difficult · 591 words · 5 questions

Most people who hold opinions about celebrated modern buildings have never been inside them. They know them from photographs, and the photographs share a family resemblance. They are taken in the weeks after completion, before the occupants have moved in, on a day chosen for its light. Furniture, if there is any, is placed by the photographer; people, if they appear at all, are small and blurred, present to give scale rather than to show use. The building is caught at the single moment when it most resembles its architect's intention and least resembles the place it will become.

There is nothing sinister in this. The photographer is paid to show the work at its best, as a portrait painter is, and an empty room displays its proportions more clearly than a crowded one. The difficulty lies in what happens when such images become the principal medium through which architecture is judged. Reputations are made in magazines and, increasingly, on screens, and they are made quickly, long before anyone knows whether the roof leaks, whether the offices overheat in summer, or whether the grand atrium is a pleasure to cross on a wet Monday morning. Critics, who seldom have the time or the budget to return, write from the same images, and the first account of a building tends to become the account that is repeated. A building's reputation is thus fixed at the very moment when least is known about it.

The effect is not confined to critics. Architects are not naive about how their work will be seen, and a profession whose rewards follow the photograph will tend, without anyone deciding it, to design for the photograph. Qualities that register in a single frame, a dramatic cantilever, a pure white surface, a striking silhouette against the sky, come to be favoured over qualities that can be experienced only over time or through the other senses: the way a staircase feels under tired feet, the acoustics of a dining room, the dignity with which a material ages. White render photographs beautifully and often weathers badly; a brick that looks dull on the day of opening may look better in its fiftieth year than in its first.

Defenders of the photograph reply that it is the only way most of us can see buildings in distant cities, and that the alternative, judging only what one has personally visited, would make criticism parochial. They add that many buildings which photograph well also work well, and that architects idealised their work in drawings long before the camera existed. Each of these points is fair, yet none of them reaches the central point. The complaint is not that photographs flatter, which drawings did too, but that the moment of photographing has become the moment of judgement, and that the qualities a photograph can register have quietly become the qualities that count.

One modest remedy would be to make the second look as normal as the first. A building photographed and discussed again after ten years of use, with its occupants in it and its materials weathered, would be judged on evidence that the opening day cannot supply. Such a practice would not abolish the glamour of the new, and it is not meant to. It would simply give the slower virtues of a building a chance to be seen by those who will never walk through it, and it would give architects a reason to care about the tenth year as much as the first, since that is when they would next be judged.

CAT-VARC-P07-040 · Architecture, design and craft · passage Difficult · 599 words · 5 questions

Architects tend to speak of a building as finished on the day it is handed over, and most architectural criticism shares the assumption. For its occupants, however, the handover is a beginning. Within a few years the first partitions have gone up or come down, a porch has been enclosed, a garage has become a bedroom, and the building has started to diverge from its drawings. A family grows and then shrinks, a business expands into the floor above, a school changes the way it teaches, and each of these shifts leaves its mark on the fabric. Over a century the divergence can be so complete that little of the original arrangement survives except the outer walls and the structural frame. If we want to know how well a building has been designed, the question of how it absorbs such changes deserves at least as much attention as the question of how it looked when new.

The obvious response is to design for change, and for several decades architects attempted exactly that. Offices and schools were built as open floors with movable partitions, and houses with walls that could slide, fold or be unbolted, so that occupants could rearrange the interior at will. The results were often disappointing. Movable partitions were seldom moved, because moving them was laborious and disturbed the services concealed within them; the flexible plan turned out to be a plan that suited no particular use well; and the occupants, rather than enjoying their freedom, filled the space with improvised screens and cabinets to make the rooms that the design had declined to provide. Designing for every possible use, it emerged, had been a way of designing for none.

The buildings that have in fact proved most adaptable are frequently ones that were never designed with change in mind. Old warehouses and mills, with their regular grids of columns, generous ceiling heights and thick, plain walls, have been turned into flats, studios, schools and offices with surprising ease. Terraced houses built for one family have been divided into apartments and, a generation later, joined up again. What these buildings share is not flexibility in the engineered sense but a kind of slack: dimensions a little larger than any one use required, a structure simple enough to be understood by ordinary builders, and surfaces that can be cut into without catastrophe. The ordinary builder called in to knock two rooms together can see at once where the loads run and what may safely be taken away. They fit their first use loosely, and that looseness is what lets them fit their later uses at all.

The lesson is an uncomfortable one for a profession that prides itself on precision. A building tailored exactly to its brief, every room sized to its programme and every service routed to suit it, is admirable on the day of opening and brittle thereafter, because the brief will change and the building cannot. Generosity of this kind costs something at the outset, in floor area and in height, and clients are seldom eager to pay for space that nobody has yet asked for. This is not an argument against all precision; an operating theatre or a laboratory must fit its use closely, and a little slack in the wrong place is merely waste. But for most ordinary buildings, which will outlive the purposes for which they were commissioned, the more durable virtue is a certain generosity, together with a willingness on the architect's part to regard the occupants' later alterations not as defacement but as the building continuing to do its job.

CAT-VARC-P07-040-Q1 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
As used in paragraph 3, 'slack' most nearly means
  1. (A)carelessness in construction that leaves a building unfinished or poorly fitted for its purpose.
  2. (B)a period of low demand during which an old building stands empty before it is converted.
  3. (C)spare capacity in a building's dimensions and structure beyond what its first use demanded.
  4. (D)the freedom given to occupants to move partitions and walls to wherever they please.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 defines the term with a colon: 'dimensions a little larger than any one use required, a structure simple enough to be understood by ordinary builders, and surfaces that can be cut into without catastrophe'.

Why not (A): The author praises slack as the source of adaptability; it is not presented as a fault of workmanship.

Why not (B): The passage defines slack by listing physical features: larger dimensions, simple structure, surfaces that can be cut into.

Why not (D): This is 'flexibility in the engineered sense', which paragraph 3 explicitly contrasts with slack.

Common misconception: Equating slack with the engineered flexibility that the passage sets it against.

Difficulty: The tempting wrong option describes the very idea the term is contrasted with. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-040-Q2 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
It can be inferred that, in the author's view, an old mill and a flexible-plan office differ chiefly in that
  1. (A)the mill fitted its original use exactly, whereas the flexible-plan office failed to fit any particular use at all.

CAT-VARC-P07-040-Q3 · Application / analogy · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following is most closely analogous to what the author calls loose fit?

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 3 describes buildings that 'fit their first use loosely' and have spare dimensions and fabric 'that can be cut into without catastrophe', so ordinary builders can adapt them. A suit with generous seams fits now and can be altered later by ordinary tailoring.

Why not (A): This parallels the flexible plan of paragraph 2, with movable parts that are 'seldom moved', which the author contrasts with loose fit.

Why not (C): Loose fit suits its first use and leaves room to alter; this coat fits nobody and cannot be altered, so it shares neither feature.

Why not (D): This is the exact fit of paragraph 4, 'admirable on the day of opening and brittle thereafter'.

CAT-VARC-P07-040-Q5 · Author's tone / attitude · MediumMCQ

CAT-VARC-P07-041 · Taste and popular culture · passage Easy · 473 words · 4 questions

It is tempting to define kitsch as art that is badly made, but this will not do. Many objects that everyone recognises as kitsch are made with considerable skill: the porcelain shepherdess has been painted with a steady hand, the sunset over the lake is rendered with careful attention to the gradations of orange and violet, and the greeting-card verse scans perfectly. Conversely, a great deal of clumsy work, the amateur's lopsided watercolour or the child's drawing of a house, is not kitsch at all. Whatever makes an object kitsch, then, it is not a lack of craft.

A more promising account looks not at the object alone but at the transaction between the object and its viewer. Kitsch offers a feeling that has been decided in advance. The weeping clown, the kitten in a basket, the soldier embracing his mother on a railway platform: each announces the emotion it expects, and the viewer supplies that emotion without needing to look closely at anything. The image functions less as something to be seen than as a switch to be pressed. What is missing is not emotion, of which there is plenty, but resistance: nothing in the work pushes back against the viewer, complicates the feeling, or asks to be noticed for its own sake. A serious painting of grief may also move us to tears, but it usually makes us work for them, by showing grief in a particular face that does not quite match our expectation of what grief looks like.

This account explains why kitsch is so often associated with sentimentality, and also why the association is not quite exact. Sentiment as such is not the problem; a good deal of great art is openly sentimental. The problem is sentiment that has been detached from attention, so that the feeling comes first and the object is merely its occasion. The account also explains why kitsch tends to flatter. Because the emotion it calls up is one we already possess and approve of, tenderness towards the innocent or pride in sacrifice, we come away feeling good not about the work but about ourselves.

None of this requires us to despise those who enjoy kitsch, or to pretend that we never do. A cheap figurine on a grandmother's shelf may carry real memories, and those memories are not made false by the object's lack of ambition. Comfort is a legitimate thing to want from an object. The danger arises only when the habit of ready-made feeling spreads from the mantelpiece to places where attention matters more: to the way we respond to news of distant suffering, say, or to the way public ceremonies ask us to feel about history. There, the switch that kitsch trains us to press can stand in for the harder work of finding out what actually happened.

CAT-VARC-P07-041-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a lack of craft cannot be what makes an object kitsch because
  1. (A)judgements of craft vary so widely between viewers that they cannot ground any definition.
  2. (B)clumsy work usually provokes stronger feelings in its viewers than skilful work does.
  3. (C)most kitsch objects are produced by trained artists rather than by amateurs or children.
  4. (D)skilfully made objects can be kitsch, while much clumsy work is not.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 1 gives skilful kitsch (the shepherdess, the sunset, the verse that 'scans perfectly') and clumsy work that 'is not kitsch at all', and concludes that whatever makes an object kitsch 'it is not a lack of craft'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 never says that judgements of craft are unreliable; it treats the skill of the shepherdess and the clumsiness of the watercolour as plain facts.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 makes no comparison of the feelings that clumsy and skilful work provoke; its argument rests only on which objects count as kitsch.

Why not (C): Paragraph 1 says many kitsch objects are made 'with considerable skill', not that most are made by trained artists; the claim about who makes them is the option's addition.

Common misconception: Reading in a general scepticism about craft, or turning many skilful kitsch objects into a claim about who usually makes them.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in paragraph 1 and the wrong options add claims absent from it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Why not (B): No ranking of genres is made; the example contrasts a demanding treatment of feeling with a ready-made one, whatever the subject.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 says such a painting 'may also move us to tears'; the contrast is in how the tears are earned, so the option reverses the point.

CAT-VARC-P07-042 · Taste and popular culture · passage Easy · 409 words · 4 questions

Few rules of modern etiquette are enforced as fiercely as the ban on spoilers. A friend who reveals the ending of a film that you have not yet seen is treated as though he had stolen something from you, and online discussions of new releases now arrive wrapped in warnings. Behind the etiquette lies a theory about what stories are for. On this view, the value of a narrative is concentrated in the not-knowing, and once the outcome is known the thing is used up, like a lottery ticket that has already been scratched.

The theory is difficult to square with how people actually behave. Children demand the same bedtime story night after night and object loudly if a single word is changed. Adults rewatch favourite films until they can recite the dialogue, and they reread novels whose endings they could describe in a sentence. Audiences at tragedies drawn from well-known legends have always known that the hero will die; for centuries this knowledge did not stop them from attending, and it may even have been part of the attraction. If knowing the ending destroyed the pleasure, none of this would make sense.

What these cases suggest is that suspense is not simply uncertainty about outcome. A viewer can know perfectly well that the hero survives and still grip the arm of the chair when the rope begins to fray, because attention is absorbed in the unfolding moment rather than in a calculation about the final scene. Much of what we enjoy in a story, moreover, lies in the manner of its telling: the rhythm of a scene, the turn of a phrase, the way a minor character reveals himself in a single gesture. None of this is spoiled by a summary of the plot, since a summary is precisely what leaves it out.

It would be too much to conclude that spoilers do no harm at all. Some stories are built around a single reversal, and the shock of the first encounter with it is a real pleasure that cannot be had twice; a mystery whose solution has been announced in advance loses something that rereading does not restore. The etiquette, then, protects something genuine. But what it protects is a narrow and unrepeatable pleasure, not the whole value of a story, and a culture that treats the ending as the only thing worth guarding may gradually forget how to enjoy everything that comes before it.

CAT-VARC-P07-042-Q1 · Main idea / primary purpose · EasyMCQ
The passage is primarily concerned with
  1. (A)explaining why children, unlike adults, enjoy hearing the same story told many times over.
  2. (B)questioning the belief that knowing a story's outcome destroys its value, while granting that something real is lost.
  3. (C)arguing that stories built around a single surprise are inferior to stories that reward rereading.
  4. (D)defending the etiquette against spoilers as the necessary protection of what gives every story its value for an audience.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 states the theory that a known story is 'used up', paragraphs 2 and 3 argue against it, and paragraph 4 concedes that some stories lose a genuine but narrow pleasure. The correct answer captures both the critique and the concession.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 gives children and adults as parallel cases of the same behaviour, and the point is only one piece of evidence, so the option is both distorted and too narrow.

Why not (C): The author never ranks the two kinds of story; paragraph 4 treats the shock of a reversal as 'a real pleasure', not as a mark of inferiority.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says the etiquette protects only 'a narrow and unrepeatable pleasure, not the whole value of a story', so the option inflates the concession into the thesis.

Common misconception: Choosing the defence of the etiquette because paragraph 4 says it 'protects something genuine'.

Difficulty: The structure of the passage is simple and the concession is clearly marked as limited. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-043 · Taste and popular culture · passage Medium · 410 words · 4 questions

The phrase 'guilty pleasure' has become one of the most common ways of talking about taste. A person confesses to loving a televised singing contest, a formulaic romance series or a song that dominated the charts in her teens, and the confession is offered with a smile that asks to be forgiven. On the surface the phrase is modest, even self-deprecating; looked at more closely, it does a surprising amount of work, and not all of that work is modest.

Consider what the speaker gains. By calling the pleasure guilty, she is able to enjoy the thing while signalling that she knows it is beneath her; she occupies the position of the enthusiast and the position of the critic at once. The confession is a kind of insurance: if her listener turns out to share the enthusiasm, nothing is lost; if the listener sneers, she has already sneered first. What looks like an admission of bad taste is therefore, very often, a display of good taste, since only someone who knows the rankings can confess to violating them.

This is why the phrase, for all its apparent humility, tends to preserve the hierarchy it seems to question. Every guilty pleasure implies an innocent one, a set of books, films and songs that may be enjoyed without apology because they are approved. The confession leaves that ranking intact; indeed it needs the ranking in order to mean anything. And the ranking itself is rarely examined. It is striking how often the pleasures labelled guilty are those associated with audiences who have little cultural prestige, the teenage, the provincial, the domestic, while the pleasures of the already respected are seldom described in this way, however predictable they may be.

It does not follow that no pleasure should ever make us uneasy. Some enjoyments really do deserve scrutiny: laughter at another person's humiliation, for instance, or the thrill of watching real suffering staged for entertainment. But in those cases the unease concerns what the pleasure does or endorses, not where its object sits in a ranking of prestige, and the honest response is to ask what we are enjoying and why. That question is worth asking of the singing contest as well. Perhaps the answer will be that it offers something real, the spectacle of ordinary people attempting something difficult in public. If so, the pleasure needs no apology; if not, a shrug and a smile will not settle the matter either.

CAT-VARC-P07-043-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to paragraph 2, confessing to a guilty pleasure works as 'a kind of insurance' because
  1. (A)it guarantees that the listener will forgive the speaker for her lapse of taste.
  2. (B)the speaker is safe whether her listener shares the enthusiasm or scorns it.
  3. (C)it allows the speaker to abandon her stated opinion later without any embarrassment.
  4. (D)it obliges the listener to confess an embarrassing enthusiasm of his own in return.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: 'if her listener turns out to share the enthusiasm, nothing is lost; if the listener sneers, she has already sneered first.' Either way the speaker is covered, which is what insurance does.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 says only that the smile 'asks to be forgiven'; paragraph 2 says the speaker is safe even if the listener sneers, so no forgiveness is guaranteed or needed.

Why not (C): Changing one's opinion later is never mentioned; the protection described concerns the listener's immediate reaction.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 imposes no obligation on the listener; it considers only two possible reactions, sharing the enthusiasm or sneering.

Common misconception: Borrowing 'asks to be forgiven' from paragraph 1 and treating a request as a guarantee.

Difficulty: The answer is in the two sentences after the phrase. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-043-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the claim in paragraph 3 that the phrase 'tends to preserve the hierarchy it seems to question'?
  1. (A)Habitually confessing a pleasure as guilty is found, over time, to make people rate that work as highly as the approved ones.
  2. (B)The works that are most often called guilty pleasures turn out in fact to be associated mainly with audiences of very little cultural prestige.
  3. (C)Many people who use the phrase do so without any conscious wish to display their own good taste to others.
  4. (D)Some enjoyments, such as laughing at another person's humiliation, really do deserve to make us uneasy.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

The claim is that the confession 'leaves that ranking intact'. Evidence that habitual use of the phrase leads people to raise the confessed works to the level of approved ones would show the phrase eroding the hierarchy, directly weakening the claim.

Why not (B): This matches the observation in paragraph 3 and so supports, rather than weakens, the author's account of the ranking.

Why not (C): The claim in paragraph 3 concerns what the phrase does to the ranking, which 'needs the ranking in order to mean anything' whatever the speaker intends. Even the point of paragraph 2 is that the confession displays knowledge of the rankings, which it does whether or not the speaker wishes it to.

Why not (D): The author grants this in paragraph 4, and it concerns a different kind of unease; it leaves the claim about prestige rankings untouched.

Common misconception: Choosing the option about speakers' motives, which bears on the display argument of paragraph 2 but not on the structural claim of paragraph 3.

Difficulty: Requires separating the motive-based point of paragraph 2 from the structural claim of paragraph 3; one wrong option attacks the wrong claim. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-044 · Taste and popular culture · passage Medium · 435 words · 4 questions

It is often said that explaining a joke kills it, and anyone who has tried to rescue a failed pun by spelling out its double meaning knows that the saying is true as far as it goes. The listener nods, perhaps smiles politely, but the laugh that should have come does not. The usual inference drawn from this experience is that humour is a fragile, almost mystical thing, which analysis can only damage, and that the serious study of comedy is a contradiction in terms. The experience is real; the inference does not follow from it.

Consider why the explanation fails. A joke typically sets up one way of understanding a situation and then, in a single move, reveals that a second way was available all along. The pleasure lies largely in the listener's own discovery of the second meaning, in the sudden reorganisation that happens in his head rather than on the page. Explanation takes this work away from him. It hands over the result without the leap, much as being told the answer to a riddle deprives one of the satisfaction of solving it. The joke is not killed because it has been examined; it is killed because the examination has been done for the listener, before he could do it himself, at the very moment that was meant to be his.

Seen in this way, the failure of explained jokes tells us something about humour, namely that it depends on an active contribution from the audience, and this is exactly the kind of thing analysis can discover. There is nothing self-defeating in studying how jokes work, any more than there is in studying how riddles work. The student of comedy who analyses a routine afterwards does not expect to laugh at the analysis; he expects to understand the laugh he has already had. The two activities are simply different, and the second does not undo the first.

There is even a case for explanation in the moment, under certain conditions. A joke that turns on an unfamiliar word, a local custom or an allusion to a book the listener has not read cannot produce its leap, because one of the two meanings is not available to him at all. Here a brief explanation does not perform the leap on the listener's behalf; it only supplies the missing ground from which he can make it, and a listener who has been told what the allusion refers to will sometimes laugh a moment later, on his own. The skilled explainer, like the skilled comedian, knows how much to say and when to stop.

  2. (B)the listener is denied the chance to discover the second meaning for himself.
  3. (C)most listeners resent being told something that they were perfectly able to work out.
  4. (D)the explanation offered is usually far less clever than the joke it tries to rescue.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: 'The pleasure lies largely in the listener's own discovery of the second meaning ... Explanation takes this work away from him.' The joke dies because the examination 'has been done for the listener'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 1 reports this as the usual inference and says it 'does not follow'; the author's own explanation is different.

Why not (C): Resentment is never mentioned; paragraph 2 says the pleasure is lost, not that the listener is offended.

Why not (D): The cleverness of explanations is never compared with that of jokes; paragraph 2 locates the failure in who does the work, not in the quality of the explanation.

Common misconception: Picking the 'fragile, almost mystical' option because it is a familiar view, though the passage reports it only to reject it.

Difficulty: The answer is stated in paragraph 2; one wrong option is the view the author rejects. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-045 · Taste and popular culture · passage Medium · 433 words · 4 questions

Along many mountain roads there are small paved areas where drivers are invited to stop, and a sign, often bearing the symbol of a camera, announces that a view is available. People get out, look, photograph and drive on. The arrangement seems so natural that its assumptions pass almost unnoticed: that the beauty of a landscape is something seen, that it is best seen from a particular spot, and that the right relation between a person and the land is that of a spectator standing before a picture.

These assumptions have a history. The habit of judging terrain by whether it composes well, with a foreground, a middle distance and a far horizon arranged as a painter might arrange them, was cultivated by travellers who had learned to look at scenery through the conventions of landscape painting, and some of them carried tinted mirrors or framing devices so that the real view would more closely resemble a canvas. We no longer carry such devices, but the camera and the viewpoint sign perform much the same function: they select, frame and fix. A landscape that cannot be composed into a pleasing rectangle tends, under this regime, to count as no landscape at all.

The costs are not merely aesthetic. Places that photograph poorly, a marsh, a stretch of flat scrub, a slow brown river, are more easily dismissed as empty and therefore more easily drained, cleared or built upon, even when they are richer in life than the dramatic gorge that everyone stops to admire. The spectator's posture, moreover, shrinks what nature has to offer. The smell of wet earth, the sound of insects, the feel of a slope underfoot and the slow change of light over an afternoon cannot be gathered from a lay-by in the two minutes before the engine is restarted, and a beauty that is only looked at from a fixed point is a beauty with most of its dimensions removed.

It would be ungrateful to dismiss the viewpoint altogether. For many people a framed view is where an interest in landscape begins; the sign that says 'look here' has taught countless travellers to look at all, and a habit of attention begun at a lay-by can grow into something larger. The trouble lies not in framing as such but in treating the frame as the measure of value. An appreciation of nature adequate to its object would have to include the unspectacular and the unframeable, and it would have to be practised on foot and over time, with the whole body rather than with the eye alone.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says travellers had learned the conventions of painting; it does not say painters devised these devices, and the camera and sign certainly were not.

Why not (D): Paragraph 3 says the view is taken in during 'the two minutes before the engine is restarted', the opposite of long study.

Common misconception: Seizing on 'tinted' and assuming the likeness concerns colour.

CAT-VARC-P07-046 · Taste and popular culture · passage Medium · 435 words · 4 questions

Cuteness is usually treated as the least serious of aesthetic qualities, if it is admitted to be an aesthetic quality at all. It belongs, we suppose, to stickers, toys and advertisements, and a taste for it is regarded as something to grow out of. Yet this dismissal sits oddly with the sheer power of the response. Few experiences in the visual world are as immediate or as hard to resist as the one produced by a large-eyed, round-faced, short-limbed creature stumbling towards us; we feel it before we have decided anything, and it is remarkably similar across cultures that agree on little else in matters of taste.

Students of animal behaviour noticed long ago that the features we find cute, a large head relative to the body, big low-set eyes, softness and clumsiness, are the features of the very young, and they proposed that the response is a mechanism for securing care. The explanation is plausible, but it accounts for less than it seems to. It tells us why infants elicit tenderness; it does not tell us why we find the same tenderness stirred by a cartoon kettle with eyes, or why the response is so readily extended to objects that need no care whatever. Once detached from the infant, the cute becomes a style, and a style can be applied to anything.

What makes the style interesting is its ambivalence. The cute object presents itself as small, soft and unable to resist, and our response to it is mixed in a way that tenderness towards a real child usually is not. Alongside the wish to protect, there is often a wish to possess and to handle, the half-joking urge to squeeze or pinch what one finds adorable, which people frequently admit to with some embarrassment. The cute flatters our power as much as it summons our care: it is an image of something that will never answer back.

This is why cuteness should be taken seriously rather than either indulged or sneered at. A quality that disarms judgement so quickly is a useful instrument for anyone who wishes to make something appear harmless, whether a product, an institution or a mascot standing in for an organisation whose activities are anything but soft. None of this means that delight in a kitten is a moral failing, and the pleasure is, in most of its forms, innocent enough. But an aesthetic that works by making us feel strong and kindly at once, and that does so before thought has begun, is not trivial; its very lightness is what gives it weight.

  4. (D)appeal to us only when they belong to creatures that genuinely need care.

Why not (B): Paragraph 1 says the response is 'remarkably similar across cultures that agree on little else in matters of taste', the reverse of the option.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 stresses that the response is 'readily extended to objects that need no care whatever', so the option contradicts it.

Common misconception: Assuming that because taste varies, the response to cuteness must vary too.

CAT-VARC-P07-047 · Taste and popular culture · passage Difficult · 631 words · 5 questions

The maxim that there is no disputing about taste is usually offered as a courtesy. When two people disagree about a film or a piece of music, one of them, sensing that the conversation is about to turn sour, observes that it is all a matter of taste, and the other, relieved, agrees. The maxim seems to express a modern and admirable humility, for who is anyone to tell another person what to enjoy? But the courtesy rests on a confusion, and the confusion has consequences for the way we talk about art and, in the end, for the way we treat one another.

The confusion is between preference and judgement. If you dislike the taste of coriander and I like it, there is nothing for us to argue about. Neither of us thinks the other has missed something; we simply report different reactions, and the reports do not compete, any more than your being cold and my being warm in the same room amounts to a disagreement about the room. Disagreements about novels or buildings are rarely like this. When I say that a novel is sentimental and you say that it is moving, each of us means to be saying something about the book, and each of us expects the other to be able, at least in principle, to recognise it. We point to passages, compare the novel with others, and draw attention to what the other may have overlooked. These are the moves of people who believe there is something to be right about, even if neither of them can prove it.

The relativist replies that this behaviour, however common, rests on an illusion. There is no fact about the book's sentimentality independent of readers' reactions, the argument runs, and so our disputes, unlike disputes about the boiling point of water, can never be settled; at most one party tires and gives up. This reply assumes that a dispute is worth having only if it can end in proof. Aesthetic argument, however, does not aim at proof. It aims at a change in perception, and its success is the moment when you look again at the passage I have pointed to and hear the false note I heard, or when I, rereading it at your insistence, find that what I took for manipulation is in fact restraint. Such moments are not rare. Most people can remember a friend, a teacher or a critic who showed them something they had not been able to see, and they do not remember the experience as having been talked into a preference; they remember it as having learned something.

It must be granted that some disagreements reach a floor. Two careful readers may agree about every feature of a poem, its rhythm, its imagery, its debts and its departures, and still differ about whether it is good, because one prizes austerity and the other abundance. At this point argument may genuinely run out, and it is sensible to say so. But the floor lies far lower than the maxim implies. Most disputes about taste break off long before they reach it, not because the parties have exhausted their reasons, but because one of them has invoked the maxim in order to end the conversation.

There is, finally, something less humble in the maxim than it appears. To tell someone that their view of a novel is merely a matter of taste is to decline to treat it as a view at all, that is, as a claim that might be true and from which one might learn. The maxim protects both parties from the risk of being wrong, but it does so at the price of the possibility of being changed. Real respect for another person's judgement would consist in disputing it.

CAT-VARC-P07-047-Q1 · Specific detail · EasyMCQ
According to the passage, a disagreement about the taste of coriander differs from a disagreement about a novel in that
  1. (A)it is a judgement about the quality of a thing rather than a report of a reaction.
  2. (B)neither party to it believes that the other has missed something about what is being tasted.
  3. (C)it is far more likely to end with one of the two parties tiring and giving up.
  4. (D)it can be settled by appeal to a fact, as a dispute about boiling water can.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2: in the coriander case 'Neither of us thinks the other has missed something; we simply report different reactions'. In the novel dispute each expects the other 'to be able ... to recognise' what is being said about the book.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 says the opposite: in the coriander case 'we simply report different reactions', while judgements are what the novel dispute involves.

Why not (C): Giving up is how the relativist describes the end of aesthetic disputes in paragraph 3; the coriander case has no dispute that could end this way.

Why not (D): The boiling point appears in paragraph 3 as the relativist's contrast with all aesthetic disputes; the coriander case, says paragraph 2, involves 'nothing for us to argue about' at all.

Common misconception: Reversing the roles of the two cases, or bringing in the boiling-point contrast from paragraph 3.

Difficulty: The contrast is stated in paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-047-Q2 · Meaning in context · MediumMCQ
In paragraph 4, the statement that 'some disagreements reach a floor' means that
  1. (A)aesthetic argument can in the end yield proof of the kind that the relativist demands of it.
  2. (B)some disputes about a work become so heated that the parties refuse to continue them, though both still have reasons to give.
  3. (C)some disputes persist even when both parties agree about every feature of a work, because they value different qualities.
  4. (D)most disputes about taste end only after the parties have exhausted every reason they have.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 4 explains the floor with two careful readers who 'agree about every feature of a poem ... and still differ about whether it is good, because one prizes austerity and the other abundance'. The floor is the point where shared perception leaves a difference of values.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says aesthetic argument 'does not aim at proof', and at the floor 'argument may genuinely run out', which is no proof at all.

Why not (B): Breaking off a conversation is what paragraph 4 says happens before the floor is reached; the floor is where reasons, not tempers, run out.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 says the opposite: most disputes 'break off long before they reach it', when one party invokes the maxim.

Common misconception: Mixing up the floor with the earlier breaking off of disputes that the same paragraph criticises.

Difficulty: The figurative term is defined by the example after it, but one wrong option reuses the paragraph's own description of disputes that stop early. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-047-Q3 · Inference · MediumMCQ
The author refers to people who remember someone who 'showed them something they had not been able to see' (paragraph 3) in order to suggest that such experiences are
  1. (A)cases of being persuaded into a new preference by a more forceful or confident personality.
  2. (B)proof that there are facts about books that are wholly independent of readers' reactions.
  3. (C)rare exceptions that leave the general truth of the relativist's reply largely intact.
  4. (D)evidence that aesthetic disputes can succeed even though they cannot end in proof.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

The relativist assumes that a dispute is worth having 'only if it can end in proof'; the author replies that aesthetic argument aims at 'a change in perception' and points to remembered moments of being shown something, which people recall 'as having learned something'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 3 says people 'do not remember the experience as having been talked into a preference', so the option is the reading the author rejects.

Why not (B): The author never claims proof or independent facts; the point is that argument succeeds by changing perception, which needs no such proof.

Why not (C): Paragraph 3 says 'Such moments are not rare', and the examples are offered against the relativist's reply, not as exceptions to it.

Common misconception: Choosing the proof option, which overstates the author's reply into a claim about independent facts.

Difficulty: Requires seeing that the author's reply changes what success means rather than supplying proof. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-047-Q4 · Strengthen / weaken / assumption · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following, if true, would most weaken the author's reply to the relativist in paragraph 3?
  1. (A)Two careful readers who agree on every feature of a poem may still disagree about whether it is good.
  2. (B)Disputes about the boiling point of water have sometimes been prolonged for years by errors in measurement.
  3. (C)When people report having been shown a quality in a work, their new view usually matches that of whoever argued most confidently.
  4. (D)Many people invoke the maxim about taste mainly in order to avoid an argument that might otherwise become unpleasant for both of them.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

The reply depends on the claim that being shown something is a change in perception, remembered 'as having learned something', rather than being 'talked into a preference'. If new views simply track the most confident arguer, the experience looks like persuasion by force of personality, undermining the reply.

Why not (A): The author already grants this in paragraph 4 and insists that the floor 'lies far lower than the maxim implies', so it does not weaken the reply.

Why not (B): The boiling point is only the relativist's contrast case; difficulties in settling scientific disputes leave the author's account of aesthetic argument untouched.

Why not (D): This agrees with paragraphs 1 and 4, which describe the maxim being used to end conversations; it does not bear on whether argument changes perception.

Common misconception: Choosing the floor option because it describes a limit to argument, though the author has already conceded that limit and it does not touch paragraph 3.

Difficulty: The correct answer must be recognised as attacking the hidden premise about perception; two wrong options repeat points the author already accepts. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-047-Q5 · Structure / function · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best describes the role of the final paragraph?
  1. (A)It turns the maxim's claim to humility against it, arguing that it withholds the seriousness that respect requires.
  2. (B)It restates the distinction between preference and judgement from paragraph 2 without adding anything to it.
  3. (C)It introduces a new argument that aesthetic disputes can, after all, be settled by some form of proof.
  4. (D)It concedes that the maxim, though confused, is in the end justified by the courtesy it makes possible.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 1 presents the maxim as 'modern and admirable humility'; the final paragraph answers that it is 'less humble ... than it appears', because it declines to treat another's view 'as a claim that might be true', and concludes that real respect lies in disputing it.

Why not (B): The paragraph adds a new, ethical point about respect and the 'possibility of being changed', answering the maxim's claim to humility made in paragraph 1.

Why not (C): Proof is not mentioned in the final paragraph, and paragraph 3 has already said aesthetic argument 'does not aim at proof'.

Why not (D): The paragraph says there is 'something less humble in the maxim than it appears' and that respect 'would consist in disputing' a view, which is no concession to the maxim.

Common misconception: Choosing the restatement option because the final paragraph relies on the preference and judgement distinction, while missing the new ethical point it adds.

Difficulty: Requires connecting the last paragraph back to the first and seeing what it adds beyond paragraph 2. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-048 · Taste and popular culture · passage Difficult · 613 words · 5 questions

When readers began, in large numbers, to write and circulate their own stories about the characters of popular novels and television series, the reaction from some authors was indignation. The characters were theirs, they said; to send them on adventures their creators had never imagined, to pair them with new lovers or to bring the dead back to life was a kind of trespass. Defenders of the practice replied with equal heat that a story, once released into the world, belongs to those who love it, and that the fans who keep a fictional world alive with their own inventions have a better claim to it than the person who merely began it. Both sides, I think, have misdescribed what is at stake.

Consider first how unusual the authors' position is, historically speaking. For most of the history of storytelling, retelling was the ordinary way in which stories lived. The same heroes passed from one teller to another, gaining episodes, companions and endings along the way; a poet who took up an old legend was expected to change it, and was admired for the changes. The idea that a fictional person belongs to a single creator, and that others may not set that person in new situations, took shape alongside printing, markets for books and laws that made authorship a form of property. It is not a natural fact about stories, though it now feels like one. Fan writers, from this angle, are less the vandals of a settled order than the late heirs of a much older one.

Yet the fans' champions claim too much when they turn this history into a counter-theory of ownership. To say that a story belongs to those who love it is still to speak of belonging, merely moving the title deeds from one party to another. What the older practice suggests is rather that stories are not the sort of thing that is owned in the first place, though particular tellings are. An author can reasonably object to having her text copied and sold under someone else's name, or to having her own work altered and passed off as hers. It is much harder to see what she loses when a reader imagines her characters on another afternoon, so long as no one mistakes that afternoon for one she wrote. The distinction between a text and a world is precisely the one that both sides have blurred.

There is an irony here that deserves notice. Fan communities, which began by contesting an author's exclusive authority, have often developed authorities of their own. Long debates arise over what counts as 'canon', over which inventions are faithful and which are betrayals, and newcomers who ignore the accumulated rules can be treated with a severity that few authors would dare to show their readers. In such moments the community reproduces, on a smaller scale, the very proprietary attitude it set out to resist, as though the impulse to fix a story and guard its boundaries were not peculiar to authors but a temptation that comes with loving any story at all.

None of this settles every case, and there are surely retellings that wound in ways the distinction does not capture, as when a living author's characters are put to uses she finds degrading. But it does suggest a more useful question than the one with which the quarrel began, which asked whose story it is. The better question is what each kind of claim protects: the integrity of a particular telling, which deserves defence, or the boundaries of an imagined world, which, on the evidence of the long history of storytelling, have never been defensible for long.

CAT-VARC-P07-048-Q1 · Structure / function · MediumMCQ
Which of the following best describes the role of paragraph 2 in the passage?
  1. (A)It explains why indignation was a natural and understandable response from authors to a new practice.
  2. (B)It weakens the authors' position by showing that exclusive ownership of characters is a fairly recent idea.
  3. (C)It proves that the laws which made authorship a form of property were mistaken from the beginning.
  4. (D)It shows that fan writing is superior to modern authorship, since it continues a far older practice.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 2 opens 'Consider first how unusual the authors' position is, historically speaking' and argues that the idea that a character belongs to one creator 'took shape alongside printing ...' and 'is not a natural fact about stories'. Its role is to undercut the authors' claim.

Why not (A): The paragraph argues that the authors' position is historically 'unusual', which undercuts rather than explains their indignation.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says only that the idea of single ownership 'took shape alongside' such laws and is 'not a natural fact'; it passes no verdict on the laws.

Why not (D): Paragraph 2 calls fan writers 'late heirs' of an older order but ranks nothing; superiority is the option's addition.

Common misconception: Treating 'late heirs of a much older one' as a ranking, or reading a historical observation as a verdict on law.

Difficulty: The paragraph's role is signalled by its opening sentence, but two wrong options overstate its conclusion. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-048-Q2 · Application / analogy · MediumMCQ
On the passage's reasoning, which of the following would give an author the strongest grounds for complaint?
  1. (A)A poet retells an ancient legend that she too once retold, adding new episodes and a different ending.
  2. (B)A reader writes and circulates a story in which two of her minor characters fall in love on holiday.
  3. (C)A reader alters several chapters of the author's novel and circulates them, under the author's name, as the original text.
  4. (D)A fan community holds long debates over which events in her series ought to count as canon.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (C)

Paragraph 3 says an author 'can reasonably object ... to having her own work altered and passed off as hers'. Rewritten chapters circulated as hers violate the integrity of a particular telling, the claim the passage says 'deserves defence'.

Why not (A): Paragraph 2 presents changing an old legend as the ordinary, admired practice of storytelling, so the author has no special claim against it.

Why not (B): This is imagining her characters 'on another afternoon'; paragraph 3 says it is hard to see what the author loses, so long as no one mistakes it for her work.

Why not (D): Paragraph 4 treats such debates as an irony within fan communities; they take nothing from the author's text.

Common misconception: Choosing the love story because it changes her characters, though the passage distinguishes using a world from altering a text.

Difficulty: Requires applying the text and world distinction to four cases, two of which involve her characters. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-P07-048-Q3 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The author says that the fans' champions 'claim too much' (paragraph 3) chiefly because they
  1. (A)keep the assumption that stories can be owned and merely change who the owner is.
  2. (B)underestimate how much an author loses when readers imagine new episodes for her characters.
  3. (C)deny that an author may object to having her text copied and sold under another person's name.
  4. (D)ignore the historical evidence that retelling was once the ordinary way in which stories lived.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (A)

Paragraph 3: 'To say that a story belongs to those who love it is still to speak of belonging, merely moving the title deeds from one party to another.' The excess is keeping the idea of ownership while changing the owner.

Why not (B): Paragraph 3 says it is 'much harder to see what she loses' in such cases, so the author thinks the loss is small, not underestimated.

Why not (C): The passage nowhere says the champions deny this; it grants the objection as part of the author's own distinction, not as a point the champions reject.

Why not (D): The champions are said to 'turn this history into a counter-theory', so they use the history rather than ignore it.

Common misconception: Choosing the copying option because it appears in the same paragraph, though it is the author's concession to authors, not a charge against the champions.

Difficulty: The correct answer paraphrases a metaphor ('title deeds'); a wrong option attaches a nearby concession to the wrong party. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-048-Q4 · Inference · DifficultMCQ
The irony described in paragraph 4 lends most support to which of the following ideas?
  1. (A)Fan writing is in the end a kind of trespass, just as the indignant authors first claimed it was.
  2. (B)Authors ought to adopt the rules that fan communities have developed about what counts as canon.
  3. (C)Fan communities are, taken as a whole, more hostile to newcomers than authors have ever been to readers.
  4. (D)The urge to guard a story's boundaries comes from attachment to the story, not only from an author's interests.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (D)

Paragraph 4 ends: 'as though the impulse to fix a story and guard its boundaries were not peculiar to authors but a temptation that comes with loving any story at all.' The irony supports locating the guarding impulse in love of a story rather than in authorship.

Why not (A): The irony is that fans reproduce the proprietary attitude, not that their writing is trespass; paragraph 3 has argued that imagining a world takes nothing from an author.

Why not (B): The passage recommends nothing of the kind; paragraph 4 describes fan rules as reproducing 'the very proprietary attitude it set out to resist'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 4 says only that newcomers 'can be' treated with a severity few authors would show; an occasional case is not a verdict on fan communities as a whole against every author, and the irony concerns the source of the guarding impulse.

Common misconception: Overgeneralising the remark about severity to newcomers into a blanket comparison of fans and authors.

Difficulty: Two options pick up vivid details (severity, trespass); the correct answer needs the point of the irony as a whole. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-P07-048-Q5 · Main idea / primary purpose · DifficultMCQ
Which of the following best captures the author's position on the quarrel described in paragraph 1?
  1. (A)The fans have the better claim, since they keep a story's world alive and continue a much older tradition.
  2. (B)Each side errs, since a particular telling may rightly be protected while an imagined world was never truly ownable.
  3. (C)The authors are right to object, since laws long ago made authorship a form of property in their characters.
  4. (D)The quarrel cannot be resolved, because some retellings wound authors in ways that no distinction can capture.
Answer and explanation

Answer: (B)

Paragraph 1 says 'Both sides ... have misdescribed what is at stake'; paragraph 3 distinguishes a text, which can be owned, from a world, which cannot; paragraph 5 says the integrity of a telling 'deserves defence' while the boundaries of a world 'have never been defensible for long'.

Why not (A): This is the champions' position, which paragraph 3 says claims too much by turning this history into 'a counter-theory of ownership'.

Why not (C): Paragraph 2 says single ownership of characters is 'not a natural fact about stories' and paragraph 3 says it is hard to see what an author loses from new episodes.

Why not (D): Paragraph 5 grants that some cases are not settled by the distinction, but still offers 'a more useful question'; it does not say the quarrel cannot be resolved.

Common misconception: Inflating the concession in paragraph 5 into a claim that the dispute is hopeless.

Difficulty: Requires the whole argument; each wrong option is a position the passage reports or a concession inflated into a thesis. · Exams: CAT, XAT
